Of the fire, the sword, and the rushing of wild beasts, O glorious passion-bearers, ye were not afraid; and ye were accounted worthy of life everlasting.

Thou Who didst glorify prophets, and teachers, and the venerable, and hierarchs, and the righteous, O Lover of mankind: by their prayers save our souls.

Rank with the saints and the righteous, O Word, those whom in faith Thou hast translated from temporal things, that we may glorify Thee.

*Glory:*

We glorify the Father and the Son, and the Holy Spirit, saying: O Holy Trinity, save our souls.

*Both now:*

Blessed hast thou appeared in all generations, for truly ineffably didst thou bear God the Blessed One, O all-holy Lady.

**[Right Page — Fol. 279r]**

---

# EXAPOSTILARIA, TRIADIKA (HYMNS TO THE TRINITY), PROVENANCE MARGINALIA, AND REAR BINDING

## EXAPOSTILARIA AND EVANGELICAL STICHERA

*The Resurrectional Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera. The work of Emperor Leo.* [^285]

*First Exapostilarion:*

With the disciples let us ascend the mountain of Galilee in faith, to behold Christ saying that He received authority over the things above and below: let us learn how to teach and to baptize all the nations in the Name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, and to abide with the disciples, as He promised, unto the end of the age.

*Theotokion:*

He Who is divinely glorified in the highest by the angels, not departing ineffably from the bosom of the Father, O all-holy Virgin, and lived among those below...

**[Left Page — Fol. 279v]**

...lived: and thou, having received Him as the Mediator of salvation, from thy pure blood didst lend flesh beyond understanding unto the Redeemer: pray Him to grant deliverance from transgressions unto thy servants.

*Evangelical Sticheron 1, Tone 1:*

When the disciples were going up to the mountain for the earthly ascension, the Lord stood before them; and they worshipped Him, and they are sent forth throughout the universe under heaven to preach the resurrection from the dead and the ascension into the heavens; with whom Christ God, the Savior of our souls, promised without falsehood to abide.

*Second Exapostilarion:*

Seeing the stone rolled away, the myrrh-bearing women rejoiced; for they beheld a young man sitting in the tomb, and he said unto them: "Behold, Christ is risen! Say therefore unto Peter and to the disciples:

**[Right Page — Fol. 280r]**

Hasten to the mountain of Galilee; there shall He appear unto you, as He foretold unto His friends."

*Theotokion:*

The Sun shone forth from the tomb today as a bridegroom from his chamber, and led Hades captive, and abolished death: by the prayers of her who bare Thee, O Christ, send forth light upon us, light enlightening hearts and souls, light leading all of us inward and guiding us unto the paths of Thy commandments and unto the way of humility.

*Evangelical Sticheron 2, Tone 2:*

Unto the women who came with myrrh together with Mary, and were in perplexity how they might attain their desire, the stone appeared rolled away; and a divine young man assuaged the turmoil of their souls, for he said: "The Lord is risen; therefore hasten to Galilee unto His preachers, the disciples, and behold Him... [^286]

**[Left Page — Fol. 280v]**

...raised from the dead, as Giver of life and Lord."

*Third Exapostilarion:*

Let no one disbelieve that Christ is risen! For He appeared unto Mary, and then was seen by two walking into the country; and He appeared again unto the eleven as they sat at meat, whom He sent forth to baptize, and ascended into heaven whence He had descended, confirming their preaching with many signs.

*Theotokion:*

By essential properties, O Virgin, Thy Son awoke from sleep as one dead, and rose as God; for He saw no corruption. Entreat Him unceasingly, O pure Lady, to send down light upon us, Thy people and Thy flock: save from daytime and nighttime stumbling us who worship Him together with the Father and the Spirit, one God.

*Evangelical Sticheron 3, Tone 3:*

**[Right Page — Fol. 281r]**

When Mary Magdalene announced the Savior's resurrection from the dead and His appearance, the disciples, disbelieving, were reproached for their hardness of heart; yet, being filled with signs and wonders, they were sent forth to preach. But Thou, O Lord, didst ascend unto the Father, the primal Light; and they proclaimed the Word everywhere, confirmed by wonders. And having been enlightened through them, we glorify Thy resurrection from the dead, O Lord, Lover of mankind.

*Fourth Exapostilarion:*

Having shone forth in virtues, let us behold standing in the life-bearing tomb men in gleaming raiment; and as the myrrh-bearing women bowed their faces to the earth before Him Who ruleth the heavens, let us learn of the resurrection, and run with Peter to the life-bearing tomb... [^287]

**[Left Page — Fol. 281v]**

...and marveling at what was done, let us remain to behold Christ.

*Theotokion:*

Having become incarnate in thy womb, O Lord, willing to call back from corruption a fallen world, as He alone knoweth, He dwelt in thee, O Mother of God; and having all found salvation, we cry unto thee like the angel: Rejoice, only pure Lady, blessed among all women, for thou didst bear salvation unto the whole universe!

*Evangelical Sticheron 4, Tone 4:*

It was early morning, and the women came unto Thy tomb, O Christ, but the body desired by them was not found. Therefore, as they were in perplexity, two men stood by them in gleaming raiment, saying: "Why seek ye the living among the dead? He is risen, as He said before! Why remember ye not His words?" And believing them, they proclaimed what they had seen; but their glad tidings seemed an idle tale:

**[Right Page — Fol. 282r]**

so slumbering were the disciples still! But Peter ran and beheld, and glorified within himself Thy wonders.

*Fifth Exapostilarion:*

Christ, the Life and the Way, rose from the dead: journeying on the road with Cleopas and Luke, He was made known unto them in Emmaus in the breaking of bread; whose hearts and souls were burning when He spoke unto them on the way and expounded from the Scriptures what He had suffered; with them we also cry: "He hath appeared also unto Peter!"

*Theotokion:*

The Creator and Lord and God of all things, taking earthly flesh from thy pure blood, O all-praised Mother of God, made my corrupted nature new, leaving thee after childbearing again even as thou wast before childbirth: therefore we all praise thee in faith, crying: Rejoice, glory of the world!

*Evangelical Sticheron 5, Tone 5:* [^288]

**[Left Page — Fol. 282v]**

O how wise are Thy judgments, O Christ! How unto Peter indeed by the graveclothes alone Thou gavest to understand Thy resurrection; yet walking on the way with Luke and Cleopas, Thou didst converse with them, and speaking, didst not quickly reveal Thyself. Therefore Thou wast reproached as one journeying alone in Jerusalem, not knowing her counsel unto the end. Yet disposing all things for the benefit of the creation, Thou didst unfold the prophecies concerning Thyself, and in the blessing of the bread wast made known unto them, whose hearts even before that were kindled unto the knowledge of Thee; and they openly proclaimed Thy resurrection unto the assembled disciples: through whom have mercy upon us!

*Sixth Exapostilarion:*

Thou didst show Thyself, O Savior, as man by nature, standing in their midst when Thou didst rise from the tomb; and partaking of food, Thou didst teach the baptism of repentance:

**[Right Page — Fol. 283r]**

and straightway Thou didst ascend unto the heavenly Father, and didst promise to send the Comforter unto the disciples: O supremely divine Lover of mankind, glory to Thy rising!

*Theotokion:*

Beholding her Son risen from the dead, the all-holy Virgin was filled with joy, and praised His ineffable lovingkindness; for, not having departed from the Father's bosom, and born of thee, by His passion on the Cross He released all from corruption, and led back to life His own infant creature.

*Evangelical Sticheron 6, Tone 6:*

Truly Thou art peace, O Christ, granting Thy peace of God unto men; after the resurrection showing Thyself unto the disciples, Thou didst find them terrified, supposing that they beheld a spirit; but Thou didst quell the turmoil of their souls, showing them Thy hands and Thy feet... [^289]

**[Left Page — Fol. 283v]**

...yet while they still disbelieved, by the partaking of food and the recalling of teachings Thou didst open their mind to understand the Scriptures; and having promised them the Father's promise, and blessed them, Thou didst ascend into heaven: therefore with them we worship Thee: O Lord, glory to Thee!

*Seventh Exapostilarion:*

Saying that they had taken away the Lord, Mary ran to the tomb, and Simon Peter and the other disciple of Christ whom He loved ran with her: and both ran together, and found only the linen clothes lying within, and the napkin for the head was apart from them. Therefore they held their peace again until they beheld Christ.

*Theotokion:*

Sing of Thy countless mercy, O my Savior, for Thou didst pour Thyself out to bear and to save

**[Right Page — Fol. 284r]**

the fallen nature of earthly beings: being God, Thou wast pleased to become like me from the pure Virgin Maiden of God, and to descend into Hades, and to lead me forth thence: by her prayers, O Lord, save our souls!

*Evangelical Sticheron 7, Tone 7:*

Lo, it is dark and early: why standest thou at the tomb, O Mary, having great darkness in thy mind, asking where Jesus was laid? But look upon the disciples running together: how by the linen clothes and the napkin they came to know of the resurrection, and remembered the Scriptures concerning them! With whom and for whose sake having believed, we hymn Thee, O Christ Giver of life.

*Eighth Exapostilarion:*

Beholding two angels within the tomb, Mary was amazed; and not knowing Christ, she questioned Him as the gardener: "Sir, where hast thou laid the body of my Jesus?" But having recognized Him by His call... [^290]

**[Left Page — Fol. 284v]**

...within herself, she heard the Savior say: "Touch Me not, for I ascend unto My Father: say unto My brethren!"

*Theotokion:*

Not having left the Father's bosom, though He became incarnate of the Virgin, the Lord came down, bowing the heavens, to save me who was fallen, and desiring to snatch me from the mouth of the intelligible beast, held fast in his bonds: therefore, having been delivered, O Savior, I glorify Thy power.

*Evangelical Sticheron 8, Tone 8:*

Not without reason were Mary's warm tears poured forth: for lo, she was accounted worthy of the instruction of angels and the vision of Jesus Himself! Yet still she thought earthly thoughts, being frail: therefore she was sent away not to touch Christ. But nevertheless she was sent as a herald unto Thy disciples, bearing the glad tidings unto them,

**[Right Page — Fol. 285r]**

announcing Thine ascent unto the Father's portion: and by these things vouchsafe Thine appearance unto us also, O Master and Lord!

*Ninth Exapostilarion:*

When Thou camest in, O Master, the doors being shut, Thou didst fill the apostles with the All-Holy Spirit, breathing peace upon them, unto whom Thou saidst to bind and to loose sins; and after eight days Thou didst show Thy side and hands unto Thomas, with whom we also cry: "Thou art my Lord and my God!"

*Theotokion:*

O Birthgiver of God, pure and all-holy Virgin, Joy of the afflicted, Protection of the world, Intercessor for sinners: by thy prayers console the hearts of the sorrowful; for we the faithful have no other hope after God besides thee, O pure Mother, our hope loving mankind, and thou savest the world, O Maiden.

*Evangelical Sticheron 9, Tone 5:* [^291]

**[Left Page — Fol. 285v]**

As in the latter times, on the evening of the Sabbath, Thou didst appear unto Thy friends, O Christ, and by a wonder didst confirm a wonder: entering through shut doors, Thou didst confirm Thy resurrection from the dead! But Thou didst fill the disciples with joy, and didst bestow upon them the Holy Spirit, and gavest authority for the remission of sins; and Thou didst not leave Thomas to drown in the tempest of unbelief: therefore grant unto us true understanding and remission of transgressions, O tender-hearted Lord!

*Tenth Exapostilarion:*

At the Sea of Tiberias of old, with the sons of Zebedee, Nathanael was with Peter, and two other disciples, engaged in fishing; who at Christ's bidding cast the net on the right side, and drew out a multitude of fishes:

**[Right Page — Fol. 286r]**

whom Peter recognizing, waded toward Him. Behold, this was the third time He appeared, showing bread and fish upon the coals.

*Theotokion:*

Venerating thine honorable icon, O all-holy Lady, show us to be sons of light, and dwellers of Paradise, and partakers of the Kingdom: for we are thy suppliants and thy people, O all-pure one; let us not fall from thy protection, O Good Lady, and account us worthy of the portion of all the saved, O all-immaculate Virgin.

*Evangelical Sticheron 10, Tone 6:*

After the descent into Hades and the resurrection from the dead, grieving as was meet over parting from Thee, O Christ, the disciples returned to work, and again took to the boat and nets; and of catch there was nothing! But Thou, O Savior, didst appear as Master of all, commanding to cast the net on the right side; and immediately the word became deed... [^292]

**[Left Page — Fol. 286v]**

...swiftly, and a great multitude of fishes, and a strange supper prepared on the earth! Of which Thy disciples then partook: vouchsafe us also now to be spiritually filled, O Lord Lover of mankind!

*Eleventh Exapostilarion:*

After the divine rising, thrice the Lord said unto Peter: "Lovest thou Me?" proposing him as chief shepherd over His sheep. When Peter beheld following him him whom Jesus loved, he questioned the Master: "And what shall this man do?" "If I will," He said, "that he tarry till I come again, what is that to thee, friend Peter?"

*Theotokion:*

Rejoice! we cry unto thee, O all-holy Mother of God, for thou didst announce joy unto Eve, turning sorrow into joy, O Virgin: and fill us with thy joy, and account us worthy of the radiant glory of thy Son and the portion of all the elect,

**[Right Page — Fol. 287r]**

O all-pure one, that we all may cry unto thee: Rejoice, pure Maiden!

*Evangelical Sticheron 11, Tone 8:*

Manifesting Himself unto the disciples after the resurrection, the Savior gave unto thee, Simon, the pasturing of His sheep as a recompense for love, requiring care of the flock; therefore He said unto thee: "If thou lovest Me, Peter, feed My lambs, feed My sheep." Whereupon, straightway showing his brotherly love, he questioned concerning the other disciple: by whose prayers, O Christ, preserve Thy flock from the wolves that destroy it!

*Triadika of the days, which are chanted in the Holy Forty Days and other fasts when Alleluia is chanted; then Triadikon, Tone 1:*

In the bodily configurations of the bodiless hosts, being led up in mind to the intelligible and immaterial... [^293]

**[Left Page — Fol. 287v]**

...mind, and receiving the illumination of the thrice-holy hymn of the tri-hypostatic Godhead, cherubically we cry: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: through the intercession of Thy bodiless hosts, have mercy on us!

*Glory:*

With all the heavenly powers, cherubically sending up thrice-holy praise unto Him Who is in the highest, we cry: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: by the prayers of all Thy saints, have mercy on us!

*Both now:*

Having risen from sleep, we fall down before Thee, O Good One, and we cry unto Thee the angelic hymn, O Almighty One: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: for the sake of the Mother of God, have mercy on us!

*Exapostilarion, Tone 1:*

Thou Who hast shone forth the light, O Lord, cleanse my soul from every sin, and through the intercession of Thy bodiless hosts, have mercy on us.

*Triadika, Tone 2:*

O uncreated Nature, Creator of all, open our lips that we may proclaim Thy praise,

**[Right Page — Fol. 288r]**

crying: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God!

*Glory:*

Imitating the powers on high on earth, we offer Thee the triumphal hymn, O Good One: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: by the prayers of all Thy saints, have mercy on us!

*Both now:*

From the bed and sleep Thou hast raised me up, O Lord; enlighten my mind and heart, and open my lips to hymn Thee, O Holy Trinity: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: for the sake of the Mother of God, have mercy on us!

*Exapostilarion, Tone 2:*

Send forth Thy light everlasting, O Lord, and enlighten the secret eyes of my heart: through the intercession of Thy bodiless hosts, have mercy on us.

*Triadika, Tone 3:*

The unoriginate Father, and the co-unoriginate Son, and the co-eternal Spirit, one Godhead, daring to glorify in cherubic praise, we say: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God!

*Glory:* [^294]

**[Left Page — Fol. 288v]**

O Trinity consubstantial and undivided, Monad tri-hypostatic and co-eternal: unto Thee as God we cry the angelic hymn: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: by the prayers of all Thy saints, have mercy on us!

*Both now:*

Suddenly the Judge shall come, and each one's deeds shall be laid bare; but with fear cry out in the midnight, O my soul: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: by the prayers of the Mother of God, have mercy on us!

*Exapostilarion, Tone 3:*

Send forth Thy light, O Christ God, and enlighten my heart: through the intercession of Thy bodiless hosts, save me.

*Triadika, Tone 4:*

Thine unoriginate Father, and Thee, O Christ God, and Thy Most-Holy Spirit, daring cherubically to glorify, we say: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God!

*Glory:*

**[Right Page — Fol. 289r]**

As now the angelic ranks in the heavens, and human assemblies with fear upon the earth, we offer Thee the triumphal hymn, O Good One: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: by the prayers of all Thy saints, have mercy on us!

*Both now:*

Unto Thy noetic servants, we mortal ones offer Thee hymnody, daring to say: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: for the sake of the Mother of God, have mercy on us!

*Exapostilarion, Tone 4:*

Thou Who hast shone forth the light unto Thy world, cleanse my soul held fast in darkness from every sin: through the intercession of Thy bodiless hosts, have mercy on us.

*Triadika, Tone 5:* [^295]

It is the time of singing, and the hour for prayer: fervently let us cry unto the one God: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God!

*Glory:*

Daring to emulate Thy noetic armies...

**[Left Page — Fol. 289v]**

...armies, O unoriginate Trinity, with unworthy lips we cry unto Thee: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: by the prayers of all Thy saints, have mercy on us!

*Both now:*

Contained in the virgin womb, and not parted from the Father's bosom, with the angels receive also us, O Christ God, who cry unto Thee: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: for the sake of the Mother of God, have mercy on us!

*Exapostilarion, Tone 5:*

O Giver of light, Lord, send forth Thy light, and enlighten my heart: by the prayers of Thy bodiless hosts, save me.

*Triadika, Tone 6:*

With bodiless lips, with unceasing doxologies, the six-winged ones sing unto Thee the thrice-holy hymn, O our God; and we upon the earth with unworthy lips send up praise unto Thee: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God!

*Glory:*

**[Right Page — Fol. 290r]**

Standing before Thee with fear the Cherubim, and dreading with trembling the Seraphim, offer the thrice-holy hymn with unceasing voice; with whom also we sinners cry: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: by the prayers of all Thy saints, have mercy on us!

*Both now:*

The Trinity, a Monad in Godhead, the unconfused Unity let us glorify, and let us cry aloud the angelic hymn: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: for the sake of the Mother of God, have mercy on us!

*Exapostilarion, Tone 6:*

Through the intercession of the bodiless hosts, O Lord, send down upon our souls Thy light everlasting.

*Triadika, Tone 7:* [^296]

Unapproachable in Godhead, Who art in the Unity of the Trinity, sending up the Seraphic thrice-holy praise, with fear we cry: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God!

*Glory:*

Having cast off sluggish sleep, O my soul, and...

**[Left Page — Fol. 290v]**

...amendment toward standing show forth unto the Judge, and with fear cry out: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: by the prayers of all Thy saints, have mercy on us!

*Both now:*

Thou Who by power on high art praised cherubically, and in divine glory receivest worship from angels: receive also us sinners who cry unto Thee with compunction: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: for the sake of the Mother of God, have mercy on us!

*Exapostilarion, Tone 7:*

Through the intercession of the bodiless hosts, O Lord, send down light upon our souls, and great mercy.

*Triadika, Tone 8:*

Having our hearts in the heavens, let us emulate the angelic rank, and with fear let us fall down before the Judge, sending up the triumphal praise: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God!

*Glory:*

Not daring to gaze upon Thee, the Cherubim, flying, cry with acclamation the divine hymn of the thrice-holy voice; with whom also we cry:...

**[Right Page — Fol. 291r]**

...cry, we sinners: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: by the prayers of all Thy saints, have mercy on us!

*Both now:*

Constrained by the multitude of our transgressions, and not daring to look up to the height of heaven, bowing soul and body, with the angels we cry unto Thee: Holy, Holy, Holy art Thou, O God: for the sake of the Mother of God, have mercy on us!

*Exapostilarion, Tone 8:*

Being the Light, O Christ, enlighten me through Thyself: through the intercession of the bodiless hosts, save me.

[Ornamental typographical vignette: cruciform composition of four floral rosettes.]

*The End of the Exapostilaria and Triadika.* [^297]

**[Left Page — Fol. 291v]**

[Blank verso of the final printed leaf (Fol. 291v), reinforced with ancient paper repair strips, with pasted slips and manuscript annotations of the 17th–19th centuries.

Affixed to the lower part of the page is a rectangular paper label with a bibliographical note in 19th-century cursive ink:

"+

In Stroev's Supplement to the Descr.

of Early Printed Books No. 39 are shown 297 leaves,

whereas here by count are 295; upon the final two

probably should be the verses mentioned by him."

Pasted diagonally in the center of the leaf is a rhomboid paper fragment with 17th-century cursive pen trials: "Lord my God" and calligraphic flourishes.

In the upper part of the leaf beneath the pasted slips can be discerned faded 17th-century cursive notes and pen trials: "... by mercy ...", "... Pan gift ...", "... of the parish clerk ...", as well as ink stains and offsets.]

**[Right Page — Fol. [Rear Flyleaf]]**

[Rear Flyleaf / free endleaf (Fol. [292r]). The page is filled with ownership inscriptions, business memoranda, and calligraphic pen trials of the 17th–18th centuries.

In the upper left corner is a pencil notation: "N 2740".

Immediately below follows an inscription in 17th-century cursive in iron-gall ink:

"December 13th day. Unto Petr Sergeevich given 25; took." [^298]

Across the entire leaf are set large calligraphic monograms, initial letters with intricate flourishes and pen flourishing: a large decorative initial "A", the cursive word "Voin" (Warrior), ornamental paraphs, the cursive phrase "Az esm' v...", an extensive calligraphic cursive inscription "Demezovedchine...", and decorative avian and spiral figures formed of interlaced ink lines. In the lower corner are archival pencil numbers: "65 / 68".]

[Lower Outer Binding Board: Contemporary blind-tooled calfskin binding of the first third of the 17th century (dark brown calf leather over bevelled wooden boards). Around the perimeter of the board runs a blind-tooled framework: an outer double-fillet frame and an inner ornamental frame rolled with a Renaissance palmette and foliate roll. The central panel is unadorned, displaying natural age patina, scuffs, surface abrasions, and isolated minor worming. On the left (fore-edge) of the board are preserved the brass catches and leather strap remnants of two original clasps. On the right edge near the lower spine corner are visible the remains of an early paper shelfmark label. The leather is turned in over the inner board edges with neat diagonal mitered folds at the corners.[^299]]

# FOOTNOTES AND SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY



[^1]: The Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra preface to the reader is composed in 17th-century vernacular Chancery Ruthenian (*prosta mova*), marked by Polish syntactic borrowings (*абысь*, *кождый*, *зачимъ*) and Ukrainian phonetic developments (*буди*, *матки*, *живаго*), serving as a didactic pastoral address distinct from the archaic Church Slavonic of the Octoechos hymnody.

[^2]: Ruthenian *v"yezdě* (въездѣ): denotes pastoral itinerary, travel, or episcopal/pastoral visitation across the parishes of the Kyivan metropolitanate, or alternately one's local parish settlement and liturgical station.

[^3]: Full-page woodcut frontispiece portraying St. John of Damascus (c. 676–749), hymnographer, Father of the Church, and compiler of the Octoechos. The inscribed text is Psalm 48:4 [LXX 48:3]: "My mouth shall speak wisdom, and the meditation of my heart shall be of understanding." The initials "K D" belong to the renowned Kyivan woodcut master active at the Pechersk Lavra press in the 1620s.

[^4]: The Saturday Great Vespers Dogmatikon in Tone 1 (*Vsemirnuyu slavu* / "The glory of the whole world") is traditionally attributed to St. John of Damascus, celebrating the mystery of the Incarnation and the Virgin as the Ark and gate of heaven.

[^5]: Epigraphic witness: The Kyiv 1629 imprint reads *iz" istlěniya* (изъ истлѣ́нїа / "out of corruption"), reflecting Greek ἐκ φθορᾶς, rather than the variant reading *ot" tleniya*.

[^6]: The Sunday Midnight Office (*Polunoshchnitsa*) features a dedicated Canon to the Most Holy and Undivided Trinity for each of the Eight Tones, chanted in monastic and cathedral practice prior to Sunday Matins.

[^7]: Melodic model (*podoben* / automelon): *Grob" tvoy* (Гро́бъ тво́й / "Thy Tomb, O Savior, the soldiers guarding..."), Tone 1, providing the rhythmic and strophic template for the Sessional Hymn (Kathisma / Sedalen).

[^8]: The Sessional Theotokion highlights the maternal bold intercession (*parrhesia*) of the Theotokos before the Son, urging believers to invoke her day and night to dispel despondency and spiritual lethargy.

[^9]: The Irmos of Ode 6 draws directly from the biblical prayer of Jonah in the belly of the sea monster (Jonah 2:3–7 [LXX Jonah 2:4–8]), an archetype of descent into Hades and divine deliverance.

[^10]: The phrase *na stado yezhe okradoste* (на ста́до є́же ѡкра́досте) in the Sedalen addresses the Trinity on behalf of the faithful flock that has been raided, scattered, or despoiled by spiritual adversaries (the demons).

[^11]: The Sunday Midnight Office (*Polunoshchnitsa*) dedicated Canon to the Holy and Undivided Trinity in Tone 1 concludes here with the completion of Ode 8 and the entirety of Ode 9, ending with a final Theotokion before the transition to Sunday Matins.

[^12]: Heading `НАЧА́ЛО ОУ́ТРЕННї` (Beginning of Matins): In early 17th-century Ruthenian printed Octoechoi, Sunday Matins is printed continuously following the Midnight Office. It opens with "God is the Lord" and the Resurrection Apolytikion of the Tone (*Kameni znamenanu* / "The stone being sealed..."), accompanied by a cross-reference rubric to Saturday Great Vespers where the full text is printed.

[^13]: Cursive 19th-century ownership inscription along the lower margin of Fol. 10r: *Принадлежитъ библїотекѣ Тр. Сергїевой Лавры 1859 года* ("Belongs to the library of the Trinity-Sergius Lavra, year 1859"), witnessing to the codex's historical preservation in the library of the Trinity-St. Sergius Lavra.

[^14]: The Sunday Hypakoe (*hypakoe* / у̀пакоѝ): *Razboinicheye pokayaniye* ("The repentance of the thief opened Paradise..."), chanted after the second Kathisma and Sedalen, reflecting early Jerusalem liturgical practice.

[^15]: The Hymns of Ascent (Anavathmoi / *Stepénna*), Tone 1, consisting of three antiphons based on the Gradual Psalms (Psalms 119–133 [LXX 118–132]), composed by St. Theodore the Studite (759–826). The second antiphon opens with an allusion to Psalm 120:1 [LXX 119:1]: *V hory tvoyikh voznesé mya zakon dobrodětelei* ("Unto the mountains of Thine ordinances did the law of virtues elevate me...").

[^16]: Trinitarian theology in the third antiphon: *yedinstvo bo yest' Tritsa yestestvom", a ne litsy* ("for the Trinity is one in essence, yet not in persons"), an exact Slavonic rendering of the orthodox Cappadocian formula (μία οὐσία, τρεῖς ὑποστάσεις / one essence, three hypostases).

[^17]: Sunday Canon of the Resurrection, Tone 1, traditionally attributed to St. John of Damascus. The Irmos of Ode 1 (*Tvoyá pobědítelnaya desnítsa* / "Thy victorious right hand...") is based on the First Biblical Canticle, the Song of Moses at the Red Sea (Exodus 15:1–19 [LXX 15:1–19]).

[^18]: The Kyiv 1629 Octoechos includes alongside the Resurrection Canon two complementary Sunday canons: the Canon to the Precious and Life-Giving Cross (*In" Kanon" Krestu*) and the Canon to the Most Holy Theotokos (*In" Kanon" Presvyatěi Bogoroditsě*), chanted conjointly at Sunday Matins.

[^19]: Dogmatic Theotokion of the Canon to the Cross: *Boga dshche zachalá yesi... v" dvoyu sushchestvu, znayemago sna* ("Thou didst conceive God, O daughter... the Only-begotten Son known in two natures"), expressing the Christological dogma of the Council of Chalcedon (451 AD).

[^20]: Epigraphic reading: *u Piláta isprosíste umobrědno* [Slavonic: оумобрѣ́дно / оумнобрѣ́дно], translating Greek ἐкφρόνως ("madly, frantically, out of your mind"), describing the irrational clamor of the crowd demanding Christ's crucifixion before Pontius Pilate.

[^21]: Canon to the Cross, Ode 4: *Kto yesí krasen" iz" Edéma... ot" vinograda vosoríka* ("Who art Thou that comest fair from Edom, and this redness of garments from the vineyard of Bozrah?"), drawing directly upon the messianic and prophetic vision of Isaiah 63:1–3 [LXX 63:1–3].

[^22]: Canon to the Cross, Ode 6: *Um" syi bezstrasten" i neshchestven", priměshayetsya Khs" Bg" umu chlcheskomu* ("Being the impassible and immaterial Mind, Christ God unites Himself to the human mind"), directly echoing St. Gregory the Theologian's soteriological doctrine (Ep. 101): "For that which He has not assumed He has not healed" (τὸ γὰρ ἀπρόσληπτον, ἀθεράпеυτον).

[^23]: Canon of the Cross, Ode 7, Heirmos: *Sotrotsi vo blagochestii vospitani* ("The youths reared in piety..."), rooted in the biblical canticle of the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace (Daniel 3:19–24 [LXX Daniel 3:51–90]). The first troparion explicitly contrasts the earth cursed through the innocent blood of Abel shed by Cain (Genesis 4:10–11) with the earth blessed through sprinkling by Christ's God-flowing Blood (Hebrews 12:24).

[^24]: Canon of the Most Holy Theotokos, Ode 7: *v Adamovu kozhu obleksya* ("clothed Himself in the skin of Adam"). This draws upon the classic patristic interpretation of the "tunics of skins" (Genesis 3:21) bestowed upon the fallen protoplasts, signifying mortal and passible human nature assumed without sin by the Incarnate Logos (cf. St. Gregory the Theologian, *Oration 38 on the Theophany*, 12; St. John of Damascus, *Exposition of the Orthodox Faith*, III.1).

[^25]: Epigraphic witness on Fol. 20r: The lower margin contains an identical 19th-century cursive ownership inscription to that found on Fol. 10r (Cohort 2): *Принадлежитъ библїотекѣ Троицкой Сергїевой Лавры. 1859 года* ("Belongs to the library of the Trinity-St. Sergius Lavra, year 1859"), corroborating the provenance of this copy in the collection of the Trinity-St. Sergius Lavra.

[^26]: Canon of the Cross, Ode 8: Typological imagery of Aaron's rod that budded without cultivation (Numbers 17:8 [LXX 17:23]), prefiguring the virgin birth of Christ without male seed.

[^27]: Canon of the Resurrection, Ode 9: The Burning Bush (*he batos* / *neopalimaya kupina*) seen by the Prophet Moses on Mount Horeb (Exodus 3:2–4) as the pre-eminent Old Testament type of the Virgin Mary, whose womb contained the uncreated fire of Divinity without being consumed.

[^28]: Rubric `Нахвали́те. Стїхи̑ры. Гла́съ, а҃.` (At the Praises / *Lauds*, Tone 1): The praises of Sunday Matins in Tone 1 comprise four Resurrection Stichera, followed by three Anatolian (*vostochny*) Stichera, and conclude with an apologetic Sticheron (*Lyubomyatezhnyi rode evreiskii* / "Give ear, O riot-loving Hebrew race...") confronting the guards' testimony and the empty tomb (Matthew 27:62–66, 28:11–15).

[^29]: Rubric `Внедѣ́л[ю]. Налїтꙋргі́и, Бл҃же́ны. Гла́съ, а҃.` (On Sunday at the Divine Liturgy: The Beatitudes, Tone 1): In the Ruthenian and Byzantine liturgical synthesis, the Makarismoi at the Divine Liturgy are interspersed with eight troparia (six Resurrection troparia followed by a Triadikon and a Theotokion), each punctuated by the refrains of the Beatitudes (Matthew 5:3–12) and the Good Thief's petition (Luke 23:42).

[^30]: Rubric `Внеⷣ вече́ръ. Стїхи̑ры Покаѧ́нны. Кѵръ, І҆ѡ́сифа` (Sunday Evening at Vespers: Penitential Stichera, by Kyr Joseph): Sunday evening Vespers inaugurates the liturgical week with penitential (*katanyctic*) hymns alongside commemorations of the Bodiless Heavenly Hosts (traditionally assigned to Monday). The stichera are authored by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886). Note also the typographical error on Fol. 25r: `Чл҃коче́нче` for `Чл҃колю́бче`.

[^31]: Stichera to the Bodiless Powers, Tone 1: The hymnography systematically incorporates the nine orders of the celestial hierarchy derived from the corpus of Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite (*De Coelesti Hierarchia*), invoking Angels, Archangels, Principalities, Thrones, Dominions, Seraphim, Cherubim, Powers, and Authorities.

[^32]: Primary Landmark: Tone 2 (*Гласъ Второй*) opens on Fol. 27r with a monumental full-page ornamental woodcut headpiece bearing the inscription `ВЪ КНИГИ ОКТѠИХЪ ВСКРⷭ҇НЪ` and the explicit attribution `Творе́нїе І҆ѡа́нна . Дамаски́на` (Composed by John Damascene). It contains the first cycle of Saturday evening Great Vespers Stichera at *Lord, I have cried* (*Pervovechnomu ot Otsa* / "Come, let us worship God the Word...").

[^33]: Theotokion / Dogmatikon of Tone 2 at Saturday Great Vespers (*Preide sen' zakonnaya, blagodati prishedshi* / "The shadow of the Law passed away when grace arrived..."): St. John of Damascus's classic theological hymn expounding the transition from Old Testament types and shadows to New Testament grace and the reality of the Incarnation.

[^34]: Typology of the Exodus in the Dogmatikon of Tone 2 (*Preide sen' zakonnaya*): The hymn explicitly contrasts the shadow of the Mosaic Law with the grace of the Gospel, drawing upon the pillar of fire (Exodus 13:21–22) that led the Israelites through the wilderness, which is fulfilled and superseded by Christ, the spiritual Sun of righteousness (Malachi 4:2 [LXX 3:20]). In place of Moses the lawgiver stands Christ the Savior, effecting the redemption of mankind.

[^35]: The Aposticha Stichera of the Resurrection, Tone 2 (*Voskreseniye Tvoye, Khriste Spase*): These four hymns, traditionally attributed to St. John of Damascus, celebrate the cosmic victory of the Cross over the tree of the Garden of Eden (Genesis 3:1–6), the harrowing of Hades, the piercing of the Lord's side by the soldier's spear (John 19:34), and the empty tomb sealed by the Judean authorities (Matthew 27:62–66).

[^36]: Theotokion of the Aposticha (*O chudese novago vsekho drevnikho chudes* / "O wonder new beyond all wonders of old!"): Composed by St. John of Damascus, this venerable hymn ponders the mystery of the Virgin Mother holding in her maternal embrace the uncircumscribed God Who sustains all creation in the palm of His hand. It affirms the orthodox dogma of the Theotokos as defined at the Council of Ephesus (431 AD) and the Council of Chalcedon (451 AD).

[^37]: Sunday Midnight Office (*Mesonyktikon*), Canon to the Most Holy and Undivided Trinity (*Kanon Troichen*), Tone 2: Composed by Metrophanes, Metropolitan of Smyrna (c. 820–880; author of the Triadikos Canons of the Octoechos). The canon provides an exhaustive patristic and scholastic synthesis of Trinitarian theology, systematically articulating the consubstantiality (*homoousion*), co-eternity, and unconfused hypostatic properties of the Father (the unbegotten Source and Cause), the Son (begotten before all ages), and the Holy Spirit (eternally proceeding from the Father).

[^38]: Sessional Hymn to the Holy Trinity, Tone 2, Ode 3 (*Svyataya Troitse, pomilui ikhzhe sozda*): Chanted according to the melodic model (*prosomoion*) *Vyshnikh ishcha* ("Seeking the things on high"), entreating the consubstantial Trinity to deliver creation from fire and condemnation and grant forgiveness of sins.

[^39]: Canon to the Holy Trinity, Ode 5, Troparion 2: Moses and the vision in the Burning Bush (Exodus 3:2–14). The hymn incorporates the traditional patristic christology (patristic consensus of St. Justin Martyr, St. Irenaeus of Lyons, and St. Athanasius the Great) identifying the "Angel of the Lord" who spoke from the flame as the pre-incarnate Logos, the "Angel of Great Counsel" (LXX Isaiah 9:6), revealing the tri-hypostatic nature of the one divine Principle.

[^40]: Sunday Matins Sessional Hymns of the Resurrection, Tone 2, after the First Kathisma: *Blagoobraznyi Iosif* ("The noble Joseph..."), the classic troparion of Great and Holy Saturday (commemorating Joseph of Arimathea taking down Christ's Body, wrapping it in a clean winding sheet, and placing it in a new rock-hewn sepulcher; Matthew 27:57–60, Mark 15:43–46), used here in the Sunday Resurrection cycle to proclaim the Lord's third-day rising.

[^41]: Hypakoe of Tone 2 (*Po strasti shedshe na grob* / "After the Passion, the women having gone unto the tomb"): The Hypakoe (from the Greek *hypakoe*, "obedience" or "listening attentively") recounts the holy Myrrh-bearing women who went at early dawn to anoint the Lord's sacred Body with aromatic spices, beholding the angelic messengers who declared that Christ is not among the dead (Mark 16:1–8, Luke 24:1–10).

[^42]: The Gradual Hymns (*Anavathmoi*) of Tone 2: Structured into three antiphons inspired by the Old Testament Psalms of Ascents (Psalms 119–133 [LXX 118–132]) sung by Hebrew pilgrims journeying up to Jerusalem. Each antiphon concludes with a pneumatological troparion (*Svyatomu Dukhu...*) extolling the Holy Spirit as the sovereign Principle of life, wisdom, and sanctification alongside the Father and the Son.

[^43]: Canons of Sunday Matins, Tone 2: The classic Byzantine-Ruthenian tri-canon structure prescribed for the Resurrection Matins in the Octoechos: (1) The Canon of the Resurrection by St. John of Damascus (*Vo glubine potopi drevle* / "Of old, the all-armed power drowned..."); (2) The Canon of the Cross (*Netrennuyu neobychuyu* / "Traversing an untrodden, unaccustomed path..."); and (3) The Canon of the Most Holy Theotokos to the same heirmos model.

[^44]: Codicological Rubric Anomaly in the Canon of the Cross, Tone 2, Ode 3: The rubric printed in red cinnabar reads *Бгородиченъ* (*Theotokion*), but the liturgical hymn text itself (*Mertv sobezakonnymi vmenisya, siyaya zhenam krasen dobrotoyu, Khriste yavisya voskreseniyem*) is a Christological Resurrectional/Stavro-resurrectional troparion celebrating Christ being numbered among the lawless (Isaiah 53:12) and appearing in transcendent beauty to the holy Myrrh-bearing women upon His third-day rising.

[^45]: The Resurrection Heirmos of Ode 4, Tone 2 (*Prishestvoval ot Devy, ni khodatai ni angel*): Composed by St. John of Damascus, grounded in the Septuagint of Isaiah 63:9 ("Not an ambassador, nor an angel, but the Lord Himself saved them...") and the prophetic vision of Habakkuk (Habakkuk 3:2–3). The hymn exalts the unmediated, personal condescension of the Divine Logos in taking upon Himself true human nature from the Virgin Mother of God.

[^46]: Typology of the Tripartite Wood of the Holy Cross in Ode 5 (*Yako kedry Khriste*): The troparion evokes Christ's voluntary crucifixion upon the cypress, the pine, and the cedar (*na kyparise, i na pevge i kedre*). This draws directly from the Septuagint prophecy of Isaiah 60:13 ("The glory of Lebanon shall come unto thee, with the cypress tree, the pine tree, and the cedar together, to beautify the place of My sanctuary"), a primary patristic and hymnographic type for the construction of the Venerable Cross.

[^47]: The Cherubim and the Reopening of Paradise in Ode 6 (*Blistitelya polozhil yesi padshemu kheruvim*): Direct scriptural allusion to Genesis 3:24, where the Cherubim and the turning, flashing sword of fire guarded the way to the tree of life after Adam's expulsion. Upon the Lord's crucifixion and triumph over death, the gates of Eden swung open to welcome the repentant thief (Luke 23:43), manifesting the royal highway to eternal communion.

[^48]: The Heirmos of Ode 7, Tone 2 (*Bogoprotivnoye veleniye, bezakonuyushchago muchitelya*): Typology of the Three Holy Youths in the Babylonian furnace (Daniel 3:19–28). While the tyrannical decree of King Nebuchadnezzar raised the flame forty-nine cubits high, Christ manifested spiritual dew (*khlad dukhovnyi*) amidst the inferno to preserve His confessors, prefiguring His victorious descent into Hades to deliver the righteous souls held captive from the beginning of the world.

[^49]: Heirmos of the Canon of the Cross, Ode 8 (*Telu zlatomu nepoklonishasya tribogatyya yunosha*): Commemorating the steadfast refusal of the three holy youths (Ananias, Azarias, and Misael) to bow down before the golden colossus on the plain of Dura (Daniel 3:1–18). Gazing upon the true, living image of God, they prefigured the mystery of the Incarnation. The technical theological term *osushchestvivyisya* ("having taken substance" / Greek *ousiotheis*) confirms the orthodox dogma of the true hypostatic enfleshment of the Divine Logos.

[^50]: Typology of the Thrice-Rich Tree of the Cross on Golgotha in Ode 9 (*Yako vrai nasazhdenno vo kraniyevi Spase*): The Venerable Cross planted upon Golgotha (*Kranion* / the Place of the Skull) is hailed as the new tree of life, far surpassing the tree of life in the Garden of Eden (Genesis 2:9). Irrigated by the water and the blood that flowed from the pierced side of the Savior (John 19:34), it buds forth incorruptible life for the whole world.

[^51]: Polemical Paschal Apologetic at the Praises (*Da rekut iyudeye*): This celebrated Resurrection sticheron at the Praises directly refutes the guard deception concocted by the chief priests (Matthew 27:62–66, 28:11–15). Employing dramatic rhetorical irony, the hymn demands of the guards: if they were asleep, how could they know the disciples stole Him; and why would grave-robbers take a naked corpse while leaving the linen cloths and face veil carefully rolled up in place (John 20:5–7)?

[^52]: The Sunday Beatitudes (*Blazheny* / *Makarismoi*) of Tone 2 at the Divine Liturgy: In the Byzantine-Ruthenian liturgical tradition, the Beatitudes from the Sermon on the Mount (Matthew 5:3–12) are sung at the Little Entrance, interpolated with troparia from the Octoechos commemorating the penitent thief, the harrowing of Hades, the empty sepulcher, and the angelic greeting to the Myrrh-bearing women.

[^53]: Sunday Evening Vespers (Entering Monday): Liturgically, Sunday evening marks the transition from the festive Lord's Day of the Resurrection into the penitential rhythm of the weekly cycle. Because Monday is dedicated to the Bodiless Powers of Heaven (the holy Angels), Vespers commences with penitential stichera (*pokayanny*) imploring the mercy of the Holy Trinity, followed by stichera in honor of the Nine Angelic Choirs to the melodic model (*podoben*) *Yegda ot dreva Tya* ("When from the Tree...").

[^54]: On the Dionysian Angelology of the Stichera to the Bodiless Powers on Sunday evening (entering Monday). The hymn directly reflects the *Celestial Hierarchy* of Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, depicting the first hierarchy (Thrones, Cherubim, Seraphim) receiving the unmediated rays of the Godhead (*nekhodotaystvennymi siyanii*) and transmitting these divine illuminations down through the subordinate ranks according to their capacity and hierarchical purity (*k yerarkhichney komuzhdo chistote*).

[^55]: Codicological Transition to Tone 3 on Fol. 50r: The concluding Sunday evening office of Tone 2 ends on Fol. 49v with a decorative floral tailpiece and the catchword *Vsubotu* ("On Saturday..."). On Fol. 50r, Tone 3 opens with a magnificent woodcut headpiece and monumental vyaz lettering *ВЪ КНИГИ ВОСКРЕСНЫ* ("Into the Resurrection Books"). The text immediately commences the first Saturday Vespers sticheron on *Lord, I have cried* (*Tvoim krestom Khriste Spase*), containing the early printed typographical haplography *rozhe* for *rod zhe*.

[^56]: The Dogmatikon of Tone 3 (*Kako ne divimsya*): Attributed to St. John of Damascus, this classic Sunday Vespers Theotokion articulates the Chalcedonian Christology of the Incarnation. The Son is begotten of the Father without a mother before all ages, and born in the flesh of the Virgin Mother without a human father, undergoing neither change (*izmeneniya* / Greek *alloiōsin*), confusion (*smesheniya* / *synchysin*), nor division (*razdeleniya* / *diairesin*), but preserving intact the specific properties (*svoystvo tselo sokhransha* / *ten idiotēta sōsan*) of each nature in one divine Hypostasis.

[^57]: The Resurrection Troparion of Tone 3 (*Da veselyatsya nebesnaya*): "Let the heavens be glad and let the earth rejoice..." (Psalm 95:11 LXX). The troparion summarizes the Paschal victory through the Magnificat typology of the arm of the Lord (*sotvori derzhavu myshtseyu svoyeyu*, Luke 1:51), trampling death by death and becoming the firstborn from the dead (Colossians 1:18), delivering humanity from the belly of Hades.

[^58]: Sunday Midnight Office Canon to the Trinity by Metrophanes (Ode 1 & 3, Tone 3): Metrophanes of Smyrna (9th c.) composed the Sunday Midnight Office canons to the Holy Trinity found across the Octoechos. In Ode 1, he invokes the classical psychological analogy of St. Gregory of Nazianzus: Mind, Word, and Spirit (*Um, Slovo, i Dukh*), imaging the unoriginate begetting of the Son and the procession of the Holy Spirit. In Ode 3, he interprets Elijah commanding water to be poured three times over the wood of the sacrifice on Mount Carmel (3 Kings / 1 Kings 18:33–34) as an Old Testament type of the Thrice-Radiant Sovereignty of God.

[^59]: Botanical Trinitarian Imagery in Ode 4 (*Otrasl sogub ot Otsa*): Metrophanes develops the patristic metaphor of the Root and the Offshoots (first articulated by Tertullian and refined by the Cappadocian Fathers): the Father is the eternal Root (*koren*), from Whom spring forth the two uncreated, consubstantial shoots (*otrasli*)—the Son by begetting and the Holy Spirit by procession—blooming as co-eternal, divine flowers sharing one essence and three sovereign hypostases.

[^60]: The Hospitable Theophany to Abraham in Ode 6 (*Preselnik syi Avraam*): Allusion to Genesis 18:1–15, where Abraham the stranger/sojourner welcomed three travelers beneath the oak of Mamre. Patristic tradition, codified liturgically and iconographically (as in the classic Byzantine *Philoxenia*), views this event as an Old Testament typological revelation of the Holy Trinity—the supra-essential Godhead foreshadowed in three distinct persons under human forms.

[^61]: Trinitarian Terminology in Ode 7 (*Bozhestva zrak yedin vozveshchayem*): The troparion balances unity and distinction: "One Form/Aspect of Godhead (*zrak yedin* / Greek *morphe*) in three hypostatic and distinct properties (*vo triyekh sostavnykh i razdelnykh svoystvekh* / *en trisi hypostatikois kai diakekrimenois idiotēsi*), Father, Son, and Holy Spirit." The response echoes the Song of the Three Youths: "Blessed art Thou, O God of our fathers!" (Daniel 3:52).

[^62]: Sunday Matins Transition and First Kathisma Sessional Hymn (*Khristos ot mertvykh vosta*): Following the conclusion of the Midnight Office Triadikon canon, the text transitions directly to Sunday Matins. The Troparion at *God is the Lord* (*Da veselyatsya*) and its Theotokion are cross-referenced to Saturday Vespers (*pisa vo subotu na vecherni*). The Sessional Hymn following the first Kathisma of the Psalter celebrates Christ risen as the firstfruits of those that sleep and the firstborn of creation (1 Corinthians 15:20; Colossians 1:15).

[^63]: The Hypakoe and Anabathmoi (Antiphon 1) of Tone 3: The Hypakoe (*Udivlyaya videniyem*) presents the shining angel at the tomb telling the Myrrh-bearers: "Why seek ye the Living One in the tomb? He is risen, emptying the graves!" quoting Psalm 65:3 LXX. Antiphon 1 of the Songs of Ascent (*Stepennа*) interprets the historical return of the captivity of Zion from Babylon (Psalm 125:1 LXX) as a mystical figure of the soul's liberation from passions into life by the Divine Logos, sowing tears in the south wind to reap sheaves of joy (Psalm 125:5–6 LXX).

[^64]: The printed foliation in the Kyiv 1629 edition diverges from the physical facsimile leaf index due to unnumbered front matter: the printed recto folio number at the upper right of Page_0061 (Fol. 59r) is `н҃д` (54). In Antiphon 1, the rare Church Slavonic verb `соѡблне́таетъ` (co-illumines / shines forth together) translates Greek συμφωτίζει, expressing the consubstantial co-radiance of the Holy Spirit with the Father and the Son.

[^65]: The Sunday Matins canon complex in the 1629 Kyiv Octoechos follows the classical Byzantine tri-ode system: the Resurrection Canon by St. John of Damascus, the Canon of the Cross and Resurrection, and the Canon of the Most Holy Mother of God. Here, the Theotokion of Ode 1 articulates the classical hypostatic union formula (`бг҃ъ и҆ чл҃къ и҆стебѐ роди́сѧ`: "God and Man was born of thee").

[^66]: In Ode 3 of the Canon of the Cross and Resurrection (Fol. 61r / printed `н҃ѕ`), the typesetter printed `и҆долекны́мъ`, a typographic metathesis for `и҆дольскымъ` ("idolatrous"), contrasting the purifying wood of the Cross with heathen defilement.

[^67]: In Ode 4, Golgotha is designated by the Greek calque `на крані́евѣ` (ἐν τῷ Κρανίῳ, Luke 23:33, "the Place of the Skull"). The troparion vividly balances Christ's voluntary endurance of the spitting and blows (`ѿплева́ніе носѧ̀`, `доса́дꙋ оу҆даре́ніа терпѧ̀`) with His divine majesty as Giver of life.

[^68]: In Ode 5 of the Theotokos Canon (Fol. 63r / printed `н҃и`), the poet provides an explicit Chalcedonian dyophysite definition: `сло́во бж҃іе соста́въ соверше́нъ , водво́ю є҆стествꙋ̀ , гс҃ гдⷭ҇ь : бг҃ъ соверше́нъ , и҆ чл҃къ` ("the Word of God, a perfect Hypostasis, in two natures, Jesus the Lord: perfect God and Man").

[^69]: In Ode 6 of the Canon of the Cross and Resurrection (Fol. 63v), the reading `Се́ніи І҆ѡ́на` corresponds to the Greek ἐν τύπῳ Ἰωνᾶς ("In type Jonah..."), where `сѣ́нь` (shadow, figure, type) represents the prophetic prefiguration of the three-day resurrection.

[^70]: The Heirmos of Ode 7 invokes the Three Holy Youths in the furnace of Babylon, interpreting the miracle through the light-bearing fire of Divinity (`свѣтоно́снымъ бж҃тва̀ ѻ҆гне́мъ`). The subsequent troparion references the tearing of the Temple veil (`Раздра́сѧ цр҃ко́внаѧ свѣ́тлаѧ ѡ҆по́на`, Matthew 27:51) as the unveiling of hidden prophecy to the faithful.

[^71]: In Ode 8 of the Canon of the Resurrection, the troparion upon Fol. 65v employs the theological compound `бг҃ососта́вна пло́ть` (flesh constituted/united with God), affirming the indissoluble divine-human unity accomplished at the Incarnation.

[^72]: In Ode 8 of the Theotokos Canon, the phrase `Чи́ны мысле́ныѧ... преминꙋ́ла є҆ѝ` translates the Greek phrase ὑπερβᾶσα τὰς νοερὰς τάξεις, emphasizing that the Theotokos surpassed all ranks of the bodiless heavenly powers by bearing the Creator in her womb.

[^73]: In the concluding troparion of Ode 9 of the Theotokos Canon (Fol. 68r / printed `ѯ҃г`), the Pneumatological clause `ѿ ѿц҃а тобо́ю происходѧ̀` ("Who proceedeth from the Father through Thee") reflects the Eastern Patristic formula *per Filium* (*δι' Υἱοῦ*), prominent in the writings of St. John of Damascus (*De Fide Orthodoxa* I.8) and frequently discussed in 17th-century Kyiv theological academies.

[^74]: The printed foliation in the Kyiv 1629 edition diverges from the physical facsimile leaf index by 5 leaves: the printed recto folio number at the upper right of Page_0071 (Fol. 69r) is `ѯ҃д` (64). In the third Sunday Praises sticheron on Fol. 68v, the dramatic Easter proclamation of the angel incorporates the synoptic resurrection narratives (Luke 24:5–6; Matthew 28:7), balancing epigraphic fidelity with the vivacity of the liturgical dialogue (`нѣ́сть здѣ̀, но воста̀ ꙗ҆́кѡ речѐ`).

[^75]: The sticheron on Fol. 69v employs the rare dual/collective expression `сꙋпрꙋ́гъ же живоно́сныхъ а҆́гг҃лъ` ("a pair of life-bearing angels", reflecting Luke 24:4 and John 20:12). The subsequent sticheron contrasts the enclosing of Christ in the tomb by the Jews with the penitent thief opening paradise with his confession (`разбо́йникъ же ѿве́рзе ꙗ҆зы́комъ ра́й`), affirming Christ's simultaneous suffering on the Cross and co-enthronement with the Father.

[^76]: A major codicological error occurs in the printed foliation at quire S (gathering signature `С`): the recto folio printed mark jumps from `ѯ҃є` (65) on Fol. 70r to `о҃а` (71) on Fol. 71r, skipping folios 66–70 in error, while the hymnographic text and quire continuity are completely intact. On Fol. 71r, the Sunday evening transition to Tone 3 Penitential Stichera is marked by a typographic band ornament, opening the penitential cycle of the week.

[^77]: In the stichera to the Holy Bodiless Angels on Fol. 72r (appointed for Sunday evening Vespers anticipating Monday, dedicated to the angelic hosts), the angels are designated as "secondary lights" (`вторы́ѧ свѣ́тлости`), directly echoing the Areopagitic celestial hierarchy wherein the angelic orders reflect the primary, uncreated light of the Holy Trinity unto the created cosmos.

[^78]: Tone 4 opens on Fol. 73r (printed `о҃г` [73], recte 68) beneath a monumental foliate woodcut headpiece across the full width of the text block and a large ornamental initial `Ж`, preceded by the calligraphic title inscription `ВЪ КНИГИ ВОСКРЕСНЫ, Гла́съ , Четве́ртый` ("In the Books of the Resurrection, Tone 4"). The first sticheron celebrates the restoration of fallen human nature and the reopened ascent to heaven accomplished by the Cross.

[^79]: In the sticheron upon Fol. 74r, the typesetter set `ве́рєѧ стертерла́зъ є҆сѝ`, an apparent dittographic typographical slip for `ве́рєѧ стерлъ є҆сѝ` ("Thou hast crushed the bars [of Hades]", Psalm 106:16 / LXX Ps. 106:16), celebrating Christ's shattering of the brazen gates and iron bars of the netherworld.

[^80]: The Dogmatic Theotokion of Tone 4 on Fol. 74v (`Иже тебѐ ра́ди бг҃оѻ҆тц҃ъ пррⷦ҇къ Дв҃дъ`) articulates the royal Davidic typology (Psalm 44:10 / LXX Ps. 44:10: "The queen stood at Thy right hand") alongside the Parable of the Lost Sheep (Luke 15:4–6), depicting Christ lifting the fallen, wolf-ravaged sheep upon His shoulders to present it unto the Father.

[^81]: Fol. 76r opens the Sunday Midnight Office with the Canon to the Holy and Life-Originating Trinity in Tone 4, attributed to Metrophanes of Smyrna. The hymnography is densely dogmatic, employing precise Trinitarian terminology (`Тр҃цꙋ бг҃онача́льнꙋ дапросла́вимъ соста́вы . є҆ди́нственною же є҆стество́мъ тріе́хъ , соприсносꙋ́щнꙋ сопресто́льнꙋ`), asserting the co-eternity and consubstantiality of the three Hypostases.

[^82]: In Ode 3 of the Trinity Canon (Fol. 76v), the Hospitality of Abraham (Genesis 18) is interpreted as a manifestation of the Trinitarian Unity (`Патрїа́рхꙋ А҆враа́мꙋ є҆гда̀ ꙗ҆ви́сѧ воѡ́бразѣ мꙋ́жествѣ , тро́ичнаѧ є҆ди́нице`), affirming the patristic reading of the three angelic visitors as an epiphany of the tri-personal God.

[^83]: In Ode 5 of the Trinity Canon (Fol. 77v–78r), the poet invokes the seraphic contemplation of the divine majesty (`Разꙋмѣва́ше ѡ҆́бѣкры вседѣ́тельнаго бж҃тва̀`), employing the radiant invocation `Свѣ́те трисли́чне` ("O thrice-radiant Light"), beseeching illumination from the uncreated Fountain of light to behold the ineffable beauty of God.

[^84]: Fol. 78v–79r (Leaf p81). Sunday Midnight Office, Canon to the Holy Trinity, Tone 4 (Odes 6–7). The troparion on Fol. 78v invokes Isaiah 6:1–3 ("I saw the Lord sitting upon a throne, high and lifted up... Holy, holy, holy"), interpreting the Seraphic trisagion as a prophetic revelation of the tri-hypostatic monarchy of God. The Sessional Hymn on Fol. 78v follows the model *Вознесы́йсѧ на крⷭ҇тъ* (*Thou Who wast lifted up upon the Cross*), standard for Tone 4 sessional hymns.

[^85]: Fol. 79v–80r (Leaf p82). Sunday Midnight Office, Canon to the Holy Trinity, Tone 4 (Odes 8–9). Fol. 79v line 1 completes the cross-page clause from Fol. 79r (*прича́стїю х҃е твое́го бж҃тва* — "unto communion with Thy Godhead, O Christ"). The heirmos of Ode 8 references Daniel 6:10 / 6:22 (*Рꙋ́цѣ распросте́ръ Данїи́лъ*), and Ode 9 references the Magnificat and Incarnation (*Всѧ́къ земноро́дный*).

[^86]: Fol. 80v–81r (Leaf p83). Transition from the Sunday Midnight Office to Sunday Matins (*На ѻ҆́ꙋтрни, на Бг҃ъ Гдⷭ҇ь*). The canonical Tone 4 Resurrection Troparion (*Свѣ́тлꙋю воскрⷭ҇нїѧ проповѣ́дь* / *Having learned the radiant proclamation of the Resurrection*) is paired with the universal Sunday Tone 4 Theotokion (*Є҆́же ѿвѣ́ка оу҆тае́нное* / *The mystery hidden from the ages*). Fol. 81r records the Sessional Hymns after the First and Second Kathismata (*Воззрѣ́вше на гро́бный вхо́дъ* and *Свое́ю во́лею распѧ́тїе претерпѣ́лъ є҆сѝ*).

[^87]: Fol. 81v–82r (Leaf p84). Fol. 81v concludes the Second Kathisma with its Theotokion (*Оу҆диви́сѧ Ἰ҆ѡ́сифъ* / *Joseph was amazed*), which draws on Aaron's budding rod (Numbers 17:8) and Gideon's dew on the fleece (Judges 6:36–40) as prefigurations of the virginal conception. This is followed by the Tone 4 Hypakoe (*Па́же твое́го пресла́внаго воста́нїѧ*) and the Anabathmoi (Gradual Antiphons 1 and 2), grounded in Psalm 128 [LXX 127] and Psalm 129 [LXX 128].

[^88]: Fol. 82v–83r (Leaf p85). Fol. 82v concludes the Anabathmoi with Antiphon 3 and gives the Sunday Matins Prokeimenon (*Воскрⷭ҇нѝ Гдⷭ҇и помозѝ на́мъ*, Psalm 43:26, 1 [LXX]). The Matins Canons begin with the Resurrection Canon of Tone 4 (*Мо́рѧ чермна́го пꙋчи́нꙋ* / *Traversing the depth of the Red Sea*, referencing Exodus 14–15 and Moses' cross-figured outstretched hands defeating Amalek in Exodus 17:11–12), followed on Fol. 83r by the second Canon of the Cross and Resurrection (*Ѿве́рзꙋ оу҆ста̀ моѧ̑*).

[^89]: Fol. 83v–84r (Leaf p86). Fol. 83v introduces the third canon of Sunday Matins: the Canon to the Most Holy Theotokos in Tone 4 (*Триста́тыщѧ* / *The captains*). Ode 3 opens with the Resurrection heirmos (*Весели́тсѧ ѡ҆тебѣ̀, цр҃кви твоѧ̀ хрⷭ҇те* / 1 Kings [1 Samuel] 2:1–10). Fol. 84r provides Ode 3 of the Cross-Resurrection Canon (*Твоѧ̑ пѣ́вцы*) and the Theotokos Canon, developing the anti-demonic typology of Christ shattering the fangs of the serpent with the nails of the Cross.

[^90]: Fol. 84v–85r (Leaf p87). Fol. 84v gives Ode 3 of the Theotokos Canon (*Свы́шнихъ*), followed by Ode 4 of the Resurrection Canon (*Воздви́женна тѧ̀ ви́дѣвши цр҃кви*, based on Habakkuk 3:11). Fol. 85r contains Ode 4 of the Cross-Resurrection Canon (*Неизслѣ́дному бж҃їю совѣ́тꙋ*), presenting the theological paradox of the Law-giving God nailed to the Cross by those who had received the Law.

[^91]: Fol. 85v–86r (Leaf p88). Fol. 85v provides Ode 4 of the Theotokos Canon, affirming Christ's two distinct natures after the Incarnation (*во двѣ̀ сꙋ́щества̀, познава́етсѧ плотоно́сецъ бг҃ъ, и҆ зе́менъ превы́шше сꙋщества̀* — "known in two natures: God bearing flesh, and an earthly mortal above nature"). Fol. 86r opens Ode 5 of the Resurrection Canon (*Ты̀ ми гдⷭ҇и свѣ́тъ*, referencing Isaiah 26:9).

[^92]: Fol. 86v–87r (Leaf p89). Fol. 86v contains Ode 5 of the Cross-Resurrection Canon (*Оу҆диви́шасѧ всѧ́ческаѧ*) and Ode 5 of the Theotokos Canon (*Нн҃ѣ воста́нꙋ пррⷦ҇чески*). Fol. 87r articulates the Chalcedonian and Dyothelite teaching on Christ's true human soul, reason, and will (*слове́снꙋ и҆ ѻ҆у҆́мнꙋ, ѡ҆дꙋшевленнꙋ . дѣ́йственꙋ, хоти́телнꙋ, самовла́стнꙋ*), followed by Ode 6 of the Resurrection Canon (*Пожрꙋ́ ти со гла́сомъ хвале́нїа*, based on Jonah 2:10 [LXX]).

[^93]: Fol. 87v–88r (Leaf p90). Fol. 87v contains Ode 6 of the Cross-Resurrection Canon (*Прейдо́хомъ вогльбенны морскіѧ*). Fol. 88r completes Ode 6 and opens Ode 7 with the Heirmos *Во пе́щи а҆враа́мстїи сі́и дѣ́ти перси́дстѣй* (Daniel 3 / Song of the Three Holy Children), praising the steadfastness of the three youths in the furnace.

[^94]: Fol. 88v–89r (Leaf p91). Sunday Matins, Canons of Tone 4 (Ode 7 concluded). The Resurrection Canon completes the heirmos *Во пе́щи а҆враа́мстїи* based on Daniel 3 / Song of the Three Holy Children. In the Triadikon of the Theotokos Canon on Fol. 89r, the phrase *во трїе́хъ свѣ́твахъ* represents a rare morphological variant in Ruthenian Cyrillic typography designating the three hypostatic lights or santities of the single divine essence (*Гдⷭ҇ьство є҆ди́но чтꙋ̀*).

[^95]: Fol. 89v–90r (Leaf p92). Sunday Matins, Canons of Tone 4 (Ode 8). The Resurrection Heirmos *Рꙋ́цѣ распросте́ръ Данїи́лъ* draws upon Daniel 6:10–22 (Daniel in the lions' den) and the Song of the Three Holy Children. The troparion on Fol. 89v parallels Daniel's outstretched hands with Christ stretching out His hands upon the Cross to unite earthly and heavenly beings into one Church. Fol. 90r presents the second canon's heirmos *Ѻ҆тро́ки бл҃гочести́выѧ вопе́щи* and the imagery of the cosmic darkening at the Crucifixion (Matthew 27:45).

[^96]: Fol. 90v–91r (Leaf p93). Sunday Matins, Canons of Tone 4 (Ode 8 concluded, Ode 9 begun). Fol. 90v features the Triadikon professing the Father as the unoriginate Source (*Ѻ҆у҆ма̀ пе́рваго и҆ вино́внаго всѣ̑мъ*) and Begetter of the Word and Projector of the Spirit (*преложи́телѧ дх҃ꙋ*). Ode 9 opens on Fol. 91r with the Heirmos *Ка́мень нерꙋкосѣ́чныѧ горы̀*, drawing upon Daniel 2:34–35, 45 (the unhewn stone cut from the mountain) as the classical Marian type of the virginal conception of Christ without human seed. Fol. 91r line 16 explicitly witnesses the liturgical realia loanword *плащени́ца* (*бг҃оносныѧ плащени́ца непра́жнены*), referring to the empty grave-linens/burial shroud of the risen Lord.

[^97]: Fol. 91v–92r (Leaf p94). Sunday Matins, Canons of Tone 4 (Ode 9 concluded). The troparion on Fol. 91v interprets Golgotha typologically (*накра́нїевѣ же на тве́рдѣмъ ка́мени* — "upon the hard rock of the Place of the Skull"), contrasting the serpent's deceitful seduction of Adam in Eden with Christ dashing the ancient enemy against the rock of the Cross. Fol. 92r contains supplications for the cross-bearing ruler (*крⷭ҇тоно́снаго цр҃ѧ*) against blasphemous enemies, reflecting the standard Byzantine-Slavic imperial and regal petitions preserved in the 1629 Lavra imprint.

[^98]: Fol. 92v–93r (Leaf p95). Transition to the Praises (Lauds / *На хвали́техъ*) of Sunday Matins, Tone 4. Fol. 92v records the first four Resurrection Stichera (*Ст҃ры воскр҃ . гла́ д҃*), beginning with *И҆́же распѧ́тїе претерпѣ́въ* and celebrating Christ's liberation of human nature from the demonic tyrant and the ancient curse. Fol. 93r introduces the Anatolian Stichera (*И҆́ны Стїхи̑ры, Восто́чны*), developing the Dyophysite paradox of Christ suffering and dying as man while rising and shattering Hades as God.

[^99]: Fol. 93v–94r (Leaf p96). Conclusion of Matins and opening of the Sunday Divine Liturgy Beatitudes (*В недѣ́лю на бл҃же́нна, Гла́съ д҃*). Fol. 93v contains the dramatic apologetic apostrophe against the unbelieving guards and authorities (*Гдѣ̀ є҆́стъ і҆́съ є҆го́же вомнѣ́нїсте стрещѝ; рцѣ́те і҆ꙋде́е*), asserting that even if human witnesses kept silence, the rolled-away stone of the sepulchre would cry aloud. Fol. 94r opens the Beatitude troparia with the classic liturgical antithesis between the tree of the Fall and the tree of the Cross (*Дре́ва ра́ди А҆да́мъ ра́ѧ бы́сть и҆зселе́нъ, дре́ва ра́ди крⷭ҇тнаго разбо́йникъ вра́й всели́сѧ*).

[^100]: Fol. 94v–95r (Leaf p97). Sunday Beatitudes of Tone 4 concluded. Fol. 94v continues the troparia commemorating the holy Myrrh-bearing women arriving at the tomb with tears and departing with joy (*ра́дꙋйтесѧ*). Fol. 95r commemorates the post-Resurrection appearance of Christ unto the Apostles and the tactile verification of the wounds by the Apostle Thomas (*невѣ́рꙋющаго ѳѡмꙋ̀, ꙗ҆́звъ показа́нїемъ оу҆вѣ́рѧѧ*). The Beatitudes conclude with a Trinitarian doxology and Marian theotokion.

[^101]: Fol. 95v–96r (Leaf p98). Transition to Sunday Vespers (Sunday Evening / *В недѣ́лю ве́черъ*). Fol. 95v concludes the Sunday Beatitude Theotokion and introduces the Penitential Stichera at "Lord, I have cried" (*на Гдⷭ҇и возва́хъ, Стїхи̑ры покаѧ́нны*) in Tone 4, following the prosomoion model *Да́лъ є҆сѝ зна́менїе*. The penitent invokes biblical archetypes of repentance: the thief, the prodigal son, King Manasseh, the sinful woman/harlot, the persecutor Saul/Paul, the publican, and the blasphemer/apostate (*ѡ҆ме́тникъ*). Fol. 96r introduces the Stichera to the Bodiless Powers (*И҆́ны стїхи̑ры Безпло́тнымъ а҆́ггеломъ*), modeled on *Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ*.

[^102]: Fol. 96v–97r (Leaf p99). Sunday Evening, Stichera to the Bodiless Powers and Penitential Aposticha in Tone 4. Fol. 96v outlines the Pseudo-Dionysian celestial hierarchy: the Seraphim, Cherubim, and Thrones partaking directly of the divine radiance, followed by Dominions, Principalities, Powers, Archangels, and Angels. Fol. 97r opens the Aposticha (*На стихо́внѣ, Стїхи̑ры покаѧ́нны*) with the poignant penitential prayer *Хотѣ́хъ слеза́ми мои́ми ѡ҆мы́ти мои́хъ прегрѣше́нїй гдⷭ҇и рꙋкописа́нїе*, invoking Christ as the Harbor for the storm-tossed and the Physician of souls.

[^103]: Fol. 97v–98r (Leaf p100). Conclusion of Tone 4 and formal opening of Tone 5 (*Гла́съ Пѧ́тый*). Fol. 97v concludes the Sunday Evening office with a Martyrikon and an extensive Marian Akathist-style salutation hymn (*Ра́дꙋйсѧ свѣ́та ѡ҆́блаче...*). Fol. 98r marks a major structural division of the codex with a monumental ornamental woodcut headpiece, the vyaz title banner *ВЪ КНИГИ ВОСКРⷭ҇НЫ [ОКТѠИХЪ]*, the heading *Гла́съ Пѧ́тый*, a decorated woodcut initial *Ч*, and the opening Lord, I have cried Resurrection Stichera of Tone 5 (*Че́стнымъ твои́мъ крⷭ҇то́мъ х҃е дїа́вола посрами́лъ є҆сѝ*).

[^104]: Fol. 98v–99r (Leaf p101). Saturday Vespers, "Lord, I have cried," Stichera of Tone 5 (concluded). The closing stichera draw upon the Harrowing of Hades and Isaiah 49:9 ("Saying to them that are in bonds: Go forth; and to them that are in darkness: Show yourselves"). Fol. 98v witnesses the spelling *ѡ҆кры́йтесѧ* (*открыйтеся*), and Fol. 99r records the polemical stichera refuting the bribery of the Roman guard (*кꙋсто́дїа*, Matthew 28:11–15) by noting the absurdity of stealing an anointed, naked corpse while leaving the burial linens behind.

[^105]: Fol. 99v–100r (Leaf p102). Saturday Vespers, Tone 5 (Dogmatikon Theotokion and Aposticha). Fol. 99v contains the classic Dogmatikon *Въ чермнѣ́мъ мо́ри* (*In the Red Sea*), typologically contrasting Moses dividing the sea with the Archangel Gabriel announcing the virginal conception of Emmanuel, and Israel passing dryshod with Mary remaining an incorrupt virgin. Fol. 100r opens the Aposticha celebrating Christ's pierced side pouring forth streams of remission, life, and salvation.

[^106]: Fol. 100v–101r (Leaf p103). Conclusion of Saturday Vespers and opening of the Sunday Midnight Office in Tone 5. Fol. 100v contains the Resurrection Troparion *Собезнача́лное сло́во* (*The Co-unoriginate Word*) and the Marian dismissal theotokion *Цр҃кви и҆ вра́та сꙋ́щи*. Fol. 101r begins the Midnight Office Canon to the Holy and Life-Originating Trinity with the Ode 1 heirmos incipit *Конѧ и҆ всадника* (*Horse and rider*, Exodus 15:1 / Ode 1 of Moses), employing the compound theological epithets *трислꙋ́чнаго* (*thrice-radiant*) and *є҆динстве́ннаго* (*unitary*).

[^107]: Fol. 101v–102r (Leaf p104). Sunday Midnight Office, Canon to the Holy Trinity, Tone 5 (Odes 1–3). Fol. 101v witnesses the metaphor of divine compunction as *бж҃тве́нное пи́во твое́ѧ любвѐ* (*the divine drink of Thy love*, Greek πόμα) and invokes the Gideon-fleece typology (Judges 6:37–40; Psalm 71:6 [LXX 71:6 / MT 72:6]: *ꙗ҆́кѡ же на̀ рꙋно̀ сни́де безшꙋ́ма*). Fol. 102r features the theophany of the Angel of Great Counsel in the burning bush (Exodus 3:2; Isaiah 9:6) and introduces the Sessional Hymn modeled on *Собезнача́лное сло́во*.

[^108]: Fol. 102v–103r (Leaf p105). Sunday Midnight Office, Canon to the Holy Trinity, Tone 5 (Odes 4–5). Fol. 102v interprets the vision of Daniel 7:9–14 (the Ancient of Days and the Son of Man coming before Him) as a prophetic revelation of the Trinity. Fol. 103r marks a codicological quire transition where gathering Ч concludes on Fol. 102v and gathering Ш begins on Fol. 103r, accompanied by the Habakkuk theophany (Habakkuk 3:3: *Гора̀ чⷭ҇тна́ѧ и҆ присѣ́нна* / *the overshadowing mountain*).

[^109]: Fol. 103v–104r (Leaf p106). Sunday Midnight Office, Canon to the Holy Trinity, Tone 5 (Odes 6–7). Fol. 103v applies Psalm 35:10 (LXX 35:10 / MT 36:9: "In Thy light shall we see light") to the trinitarian mystery of the Father's light, the Son's manifestation, and the Holy Spirit's illumination. Fol. 104r contains the Sessional Hymn weaving Psalm 6:2 ("rebuke me not in Thine anger") and Psalm 142:2 ("enter not into judgment with Thy servant") into personal penitential supplication.

[^110]: Fol. 104v–105r (Leaf p107). Sunday Midnight Office, Canon to the Holy Trinity, Tone 5 (Odes 8–9). Fol. 104v cites the Hospitality of Abraham (Genesis 18) as the primary biblical theophany revealing the Trinity under human appearance. Fol. 105r articulates the classical Eastern Orthodox pneumatological doctrine: the Holy Spirit is *животворѧ́щїй* (*life-creating*), *и҆сходѧ̀ и҆ ѻ҆ц҃а̀* (*proceeding from the Father*), and *сн҃ом же ꙗ҆влѧ́ем* (*manifested through the Son*).

[^111]: Fol. 105v–106r (Leaf p108). Conclusion of the Midnight Office and opening of Sunday Matins in Tone 5. Fol. 105v records the Seraphic Trisagion vision of Isaiah 6:1–3. Following the First and Second Kathismata (*На̀ а҃ / в҃, стїхоло́гїи*), the Sessional Hymns on Fol. 106r juxtapose the human guards keeping watch over the earthly tomb above with Christ descending below to raise the dead from the ages (*верхꙋ̀ во́ини гро́бъ стрежа́хꙋ: низꙋ́ же ѿ вѣ́ка ме́ртвыѧ воскреси́лъ є҆сѝ*).

[^112]: Fol. 106v–107r (Leaf p109). Sunday Matins, Tone 5 (Hypakoe, Anavathmoi, and Prokeimenon). Following Psalm 118 (*По непоро́чна*), Fol. 106v introduces the Hypakoe *А҆́гг҃льскимъ зра́комъ* and the three Antiphons of the Gradual Hymns (*А҆нтифо́ны*), paraphrasing the Gradual Psalms (Psalms 119–121 [LXX 119–121 / MT 120–122]). Fol. 107r marks the transition to quire Щ and records the Sunday Matins Prokeimenon in Tone 5 (Psalm 9:33 [LXX 9:33 / MT 10:12]: *Воскрⷭ҇ни гдⷭ҇и бж҃е мо́й...*).

[^113]: Fol. 107v–108r (Leaf p110). Sunday Matins, Tone 5 Canons (Resurrection, Cross-Resurrection, and Theotokos, Ode 1). Following the Matins Gospel and Psalm 50, Fol. 107v opens the Resurrection Canon of St. John of Damascus (*Конѧ̀ и҆ вса́дника*), interpreting the crown of thorns (*терконо́сное є҆ѵре́йское собо́рище*) as releasing the curse of thorns of Genesis 3:18. Fol. 108r opens the Canon of the Cross and Resurrection and the Canon to the Most Holy Theotokos.

[^114]: Fol. 108v/109r (p111). Tone 5 Sunday Matins, Canon of the Resurrection, Ode 3. The heirmos 'Оутверже́й ниначе́мъ же зе́млю' reflects Job 26:7 ('He hangeth the earth upon nothing') and the Byzantine kanon poet's synthesis of cosmology with ecclesiology: the foundation of the Church upon the immovable rock of Christ's commandments. On the right page (Fol. 109r), signature 'Щ г' marks the third recto leaf of gathering 'Щ' (quaternion), accompanied by printed folio number 'рѳ҃' (109).

[^115]: Fol. 109v/110r (p112). Tone 5 Sunday Matins, Canon of the Resurrection, Ode 4. Habakkuk's prophetic Ode (Habakkuk 3:2) is linked typologically to the wood that sweetened the bitter waters of Marah (Exodus 15:23–25), identifying the tree thrown into the waters as a prophetic type of the wood of the Cross transforming the bitterness of the ancestral transgression into spiritual sweetness. Leaf 110r bears gathering mark 'Щ д' (fourth leaf of quaternion 'Щ') and printed folio number 'рi҃' (110).

[^116]: Fol. 110v/111r (p113). Tone 5 Sunday Matins, Canon of the Cross and Resurrection, Ode 4. The imagery of the planting of the Cross upon Golgotha shattering the eternal bars ('ве́рїа') and gatekeepers ('вра́тницы вѣ́чнїи') of Hades reflects Psalm 106:16 and the harrowing narrative of the Gospel of Nicodemus. At Fol. 111r, the codex transitions to gathering 'Ъ' (marked simply 'Ъ' at the bottom recto), continuing with printed folio number 'р̃аі' (111).

[^117]: Fol. 111v/112r (p114). Tone 5 Sunday Matins, Canon of the Resurrection, Ode 6. The heirmos 'Волна́ющеесѧ бꙋ́рею дшетлѣ́нною' invokes the Jonah typological cycle (Jonah 2:3–7), framing spiritual passion as a tempestuous sea from which only Christ's descent into corruption ('тлѣ́нїе тлѣ́ющему бы́въ') can draw mankind upward into incorruption. Leaf 112r bears gathering mark 'Ъ в' and folio number 'р̃ві' (112).

[^118]: Fol. 112v/113r (p115). Tone 5 Sunday Matins, Canon of the Resurrection, Ode 7. The troparion 'Пло́тїю ѡ҆бложе́сѧ , ꙗ҆́кѡ оу҆́дицею' invokes the classic patristic metaphor of the divine fishhook (*hamus divinitatis*), developed by St. Gregory of Nyssa and St. John Damascene: Christ's humanity serves as the bait (*esca*) concealing the hook of His divinity, drawing forth the ancestral serpent (Leviathan) from his lair. Leaf 113r bears signature 'Ъ г' and folio number 'р̃гі' (113).

[^119]: Fol. 113v/114r (p116). Tone 5 Sunday Matins, Canon of the Resurrection, Ode 8. The troparion 'Ты̀ во́лною спаси́тельною стра́стїю' provides a precise theological confession of the two wills in Christ (*dyothelitism*), codified at the Sixth Ecumenical Council (Constantinople III, 680–681): Christ prayed concerning the cup in Gethsemane, demonstrating His human will operating in harmonious submission to the divine will ('двѣ́ бо порѧ́дꙋ но́сиши сꙋще́ствѣ хр҃те'). Leaf 114r is signed 'Ъ д' with folio number 'р̃ді' (114).

[^120]: Fol. 114v/115r (p117). Tone 5 Sunday Matins, Canon of the Resurrection, Ode 9. The heirmos 'И҆са́їа ликꙋ́й' (familiar from the Byzantine Crowning office and priestly ordinations) magnifies Isaiah 7:14 and Zechariah 6:12 ('whose name is the Branch / Dayspring', *Oriens* / Во́стокъ), anchoring the Incarnation of Emmanuel as the unshakeable foundation of the Resurrection. Leaf 115r marks the beginning of gathering 'Ы' (Fol. 115r, 'р̃єі').

[^121]: Fol. 115v/116r (p118). Tone 5 Sunday Matins, Canon of the Resurrection, Ode 9. The troparion 'Небг҃а безпло́тна , ни́же па́ки чл҃ка про́ста' presents an explicit Chalcedonian christological confession against Docetism and Nestorianism: the Virgin brought forth neither an incorporeal God nor a mere human, but a perfect Man and truly perfect God in one person. On Fol. 116r, the closing Theotokion applies Psalm 44:10/14 (*in fimbriis aureis* / 'рѧ́сными бж҃твенныхъ добродѣ́телей') to the Mother of God standing at the right hand of her Son. Leaf 116r is signed 'Ы в' with folio number 'р̃ѕі' (116).

[^122]: Fol. 116v/117r (p119). Tone 5 Sunday Matins, Stichera at the Praises (На хвали́те: Стихї́ри воскр҃ны). The opening sticheron contains a noteworthy epigraphic variant: 'про́йде и҆згро́ба , ꙗ҆́кѡже роди́сѧ ѿ ѻ҆ц҃а' ('He came forth from the tomb, even as He was begotten of the Father'), paralleling the eternal generation from the Father with the bodily emergence from the sealed tomb, whereas standard later Muscovite and Ruthenian editions read 'ꙗ҆́кѡже роди́сѧ ѿ бц҃ы' or 'ѿ дв҃ы' ('even as He was born of the Theotokos / Virgin'). Leaf 117r is signed 'Ы г' with folio number 'р̃зі' (117).

[^123]: Fol. 117v/118r (p120). Tone 5 Sunday Matins, Stichera at the Praises. The concluding sticheron preserves a vivid dramatic apostrophe by the Roman watchmen confronting the Jewish leaders: 'ѡ҆ бꙋ́его свемꙋ́дрнаго совѣ́та' ('O foolishness of your all-wise counsel!'), ironizing the futile attempt to seal and guard the uncircumscribed God with mortal guards and seals. Leaf 118r is signed 'Ы д' with folio number 'р̃ні' (118).

[^124]: At the conclusion of the Sunday Praises (Lauds) stichera Tone 5, the text transitions directly into the Sunday Beatitudes (*Makarizmoi*). The rubric 'Сла́, стїра є҆ѵ . И҆ нн҃ѣ , Пребл҃гве́нна' cues the Morning Gospel Stichera (*Eothinon*) and the standard Sunday Theotokion 'Преблагословенна еси, Богородице Дево' before the Great Doxology, followed by the Beatitudes ('Внеде́ли бл҃женны').

[^125]: The Sunday evening office at Vespers ('Внеде́лю ве́черъ , на Гдⷭ҇и возва́хъ') initiates the weekday cycle of Tone 5 with Penitential Stichera ('стихи̑ры покаѧ́нны') sung to the prosomoion model ('Подо́бенъ') 'Ра́дꙋйсѧ по́стническїи' (*Rejoice, thou bearer of the ascetic life*). The running header on Fol. 119v exhibits the printer's erratum 'Поклѧ́ны' for 'Покаѧ́нны'.

[^126]: On Monday liturgical services (commencing at Sunday Vespers), the Octoechos pairs penitential hymns with stichera dedicated to the Bodiless Powers (Holy Angels), reflecting the weekly liturgical allocation of Monday to the angelic hosts. Both sets of stichera share the prosomoion model 'Ра́дꙋйсѧ по́стническїи'.

[^127]: The Sunday evening Aposticha ('На̀ стихо́внѣ') features two penitential stichera followed by a Martyrion ('Мч҃нченъ') and a Theotokion ('Бг҃ородиченъ'), marking the traditional evening transition into the Monday liturgical remembrance of both repentance and the holy martyrs alongside the angelic powers.

[^128]: Folio 123r opens Tone 6 ('Гласъ Шестый') with a monumental Renaissance woodcut headpiece and ornamental calligraphic *vyaz* heading 'ВЪ КНИГИ ВОСКРЕСНЫ' (*In the Resurrection Book*), initiating the Sunday cycle of Lord, I have cried stichera (*Stichera Anastasima*). Folio 122v concludes Tone 5 with an elaborate woodcut cartouche tailpiece depicting a cherubic mascaron, below which 17th-century cursive ink marginalia records an early liturgical reader's inscription.

[^129]: The Anatolian or Eastern Stichera ('Стїхи̑ры восто́чны') of Tone 6 follow the primary Resurrection Stichera at Sunday Vespers (*Lord, I have cried*). Folio 124r culminates in the famous Dogmatic Theotokion (*Dogmatikon*) of Tone 6 attributed to St. John of Damascus ('Кто̀ тебѐ не бл҃жи́тъ'), which articulates the Chalcedonian Christological dogma of the two unconfused natures in the single Person of Christ.
