[^130]: The Sunday Aposticha of Tone 6 opens with the venerable Paschal hymn 'Воскресе́нїе твоѐ хр҃тѐ сп҃се' (*Thy Resurrection, O Christ Savior, the angels hymn in the heavens*), which also serves universally across Byzantine and Ruthenian rites as the processional troparion sung during the Paschal midnight procession before Matins.

[^131]: The Resurrection Dismissal Troparion of Tone 6 ('А҆́ггелскїѧ си̑лы на гро́бѣ твое́мъ') encapsulates the Easter morning Gospel events (the soldiers fainting, Mary Magdalene seeking Christ's body, and Hades overthrown). This is followed on Fol. 125v by the Sunday Midnight Office Canon to the Holy Trinity ('На полꙋ́нощницн , Кано́нъ прест҃ѣ́й живонача́льнѣй Тр҃ци'), highlighting the trinitarian theology of St. Paul citing Romans 11:36.

[^132]: Folio 126v contains the Trinity Sessional Hymn (*Троичен Седален*) of Tone 6 modeled on the prosomoion 'Еже о нас исполнив смотрение' (*Having fulfilled the dispensation for our sake*), borrowing the scriptural assurance of Romans 8:31: *If God be for us, who can be against us?* ('буди с нами, и никтоже на ны'). The text attributes this same consolation to the Theotokos in the corresponding Theotokion.

[^133]: The Trinity Canon of Sunday Midnight Office continues through Odes 5 and 6 on Fol. 127v–128r. The running header shifts on Fol. 127v to 'Тро́йчны' (*Triadika / Trinitarian hymns*), denoting the dedicated midnight trinitarian genre, before reverting to 'Гла́съ , ѕ҃ .' on Fol. 128r.

[^134]: Folio 128v contains the concluding troparia of Ode 6 of the Tone 6 Sunday Midnight Office Triadica Canon, followed by the Sessional Hymn (*Сѣда́ленъ*) modeled on *Є҆́же ѡ҆ на́съ* (*Fulfilling the dispensation for us*). In the opening phrase of Fol. 128v, the printed text exhibits *коперѝ*, an obvious typographical slip for *вперѝ* (from the Greek πτερώσας, "winging / raising up" toward divine love). The refrain quoted in both the Sessional hymn and its Theotokion—"I am with you, and no one is against you" (*а҆́зъ є҆́смь сва́ми , и҆ никто́же на́вы*)—draws upon Isaiah 8:10 and Romans 8:31.

[^135]: Folios 129v–130r contain Odes 8 and 9 of the Sunday Midnight Office Triadica Canon of Tone 6. The Heirmos of Ode 8 (*И҆спла́мене ст҃ымъ* / *Ἐκ φλογὸς τοῖς ὁσίοις*) evokes the furnace of Babylon, and Ode 9 (*Бг҃а чл҃комъ неꙋдо́бъ ви́дѣти* / *Θεὸν ἀνθρώποις ἰδεῖν ἀδύνατον*) provides the classic Damascene theological contemplation of the cherubic ranks veiling their faces before the unapproachable divine essence.

[^136]: Folios 130v–131r conclude the Sunday Midnight Office and mark the formal transition into Sunday Matins (*Оу҆треня*) of Tone 6. The Resurrection Troparion *А҆́гглскїа си́лы* (*The angelic powers at Thy tomb...*) is prescribed at *God is the Lord*, followed after the first Psalter reading (*По а҃ . Стїхоло́гїи*) by the Sessional Hymn depicting Mary Magdalene weeping at the empty tomb and mistaking the risen Savior for the gardener (John 20:15).

[^137]: Folios 131v–132r contain the Sunday Hypakoe (*Во́лною и҆ животво́рною тѝ сме́ртїю*), followed by the three Antiphons of the Gradual Hymns (*Степенны*) of Tone 6 and the Matins Prokeimenon. Each antiphon concludes with a pneumatological triadion celebrating the Holy Spirit as the divine source of life, deification (*ѡ҆бже́нїе*, Greek θέωσις), and regal dominion over all creation.

[^138]: Folios 132v–133r open the three Sunday Matins Canons of Tone 6 with their respective first odes: the Resurrection Canon by St. John of Damascus (Heirmos: *Ꙗ҆́кѡ посꙋ́хꙋ ходи́въ і҆ил҃ь* / *Ὡς ἐν ἠπείρῳ πεζεύσας*), the Cross-Resurrection Canon (Heirmos: *Волно́ю морско́ю* / *Κύмаτι θαλάσσης*), and the Theotokos Canon.

[^139]: Folios 133v–134r encompass Ode 3 across the three canons. The Theotokion on Fol. 133v incorporates the messianic prophecy of the Patriarch Jacob (Genesis 49:10: "The sceptre shall not depart from Judah... until Shiloh come"), as well as the patristic metaphor of Christ as the mystical Pearl engendered by the divine lightning flash (*бї́сера ѿ бж҃тве́нныѧ мо́лнїа*).

[^140]: Folios 134v–135r transition through the completion of Ode 3 and the opening of Ode 4. In the Theotokion of Ode 3 on Fol. 135r, Daniel's vision of the uncut mountain and the mystical stone (Daniel 2:34, 45) is invoked as an Old Testament type of the Virgin Conception shattering pagan idol shrines (*є҆́ллинскаѧ тре́бища*), while the Cross is hymned as the Tree of Life watering all creation with water and blood from Christ's pierced side (John 19:34).

[^141]: Folios 135v–136r contain Ode 4 troparia and the beginning of Ode 5. The hymnographic text sharply contrasts the deceit of the serpent, who falsely promised deification (*ло́жно ѡ҆боже́ні́е*, Genesis 3:5), with the true life and path to incorruption opened by Christ's Passion and Resurrection, noting that "Grace hath blossomed and the Law hath ceased" (*Бл҃гть процвѣтѐ , зако́ преста̀*).

[^142]: Folios 136v–137r present Ode 5 troparia. The hymnography depicts the cherub with the flaming sword (Genesis 3:24) withdrawing and turning his back to give passage to the redeemed, since Christ opened Paradise to the thief; Hades is graphically described as vomiting forth those whom it formerly held captive (*видѣ́въ а҆́дъ и҆зблева̀*).

[^143]: Folios 137v–138r encompass the close of Ode 5 and the troparia of Ode 6. Fol. 138r weaves together Christ tearing up the handwriting of debt against Adam (Colossians 2:14) with the parable of the Good Shepherd descending to the pit of Hades to seek the lost sheep and carrying fallen humanity upon His shoulders (*нара́мꙋ понесѐ совоскреси́лъ є҆сѝ*, Luke 15:5).

[^144]: Folios 138v–139r conclude Ode 6 of the Tone 6 Sunday Matins Canons and open Ode 7. The Theotokion of Ode 6 on Fol. 139r compiles the classical Old Testament tabernacle types of the Virgin from Exodus and Hebrews 9:4: the Ark of the Covenant (*ковче́гъ* / κιβωτός), the Table of Showbread (*тра́пеза* / τράпеζα), the golden Candlestick (*свѣ́щникъ* / λυχνία), and the golden Jar of Manna (*рꙋ́чка* / στάмνος). At the bottom of Fol. 139r, the Heirmos of Ode 7 (*Хладода́вцꙋ ᲂу҆́бѡ пе́щь* / *Δροσοβόλον μὲν τὴν κάμινον*) exhibits the curious spelling *ᲂу҆бѣ́йца* (for *ᲂу҆бѣди́въ* / *ᲂу҆бѣжда́ѧ*, persuading the tyrant to cry: "Blessed is the God of our fathers").

[^145]: Folios 139v–140r present the troparia of Ode 7 across the three Sunday Matins canons. In the first canon, the eclipse of the sun at the Crucifixion (Matthew 27:45) is coupled with Adam's ecstatic dancing (*ли́кѡствꙋѧ и҆гра́ше*) upon the Harrowing of Hades. On Fol. 140r, the Theotokion of the Cross-Resurrection canon provides a strict Chalcedonian christological formula: the Virgin bore One of the Trinity (*Є҆дина́го ѿ Тро́ицы*) Who appeared upon earth "in two natures" (*водво́ю є҆стествꙋ̀* / ἐν δύο φύσεσιν). The subsequent Theotokos canon invokes the Magnificat prophecy ("all generations shall call me blessed", Luke 1:48) alongside the typological interpretation of the burning yet unconsumed Babylonian furnace (Daniel 3) as an image of the divine fire dwelling in the Virgin's womb (*бж҃е́ственный бо ѻ҃гнь тебѐ неопалѝ всели́сѧ вътѧ̀*).

[^146]: Folios 140v–141r conclude Ode 7 and open Ode 8 of the Tone 6 Matins canons. In the Theotokion on Fol. 140v, the Virgin is called the sanctuary (*ᲂу҆свѣти́лище*) of human nature, bearing upon her arms the Lord Who sits upon the cherubim. Ode 8 of the first canon pairs the furnace of Babylon with Elijah's sacrifice upon Mount Carmel consumed by fire from heaven despite being soaked with water (3 Kings / 1 Kings 18:38). On Fol. 141r, Christ's descent into Hades is depicted as lifting fallen man from the dung-heap (*вогнои́щи*, Psalm 112:7 / 113:7 LXX: "He raises up the poor out of the dust, and lifts the needy out of the dunghill").

[^147]: Folios 141v–142r encompass the close of Ode 8 and the opening of Ode 9 of the Sunday Matins canons. In the Theotokos troparia of Ode 8 on Fol. 141v, Psalm 44:10 LXX ("At Thy right hand stood the queen in a vesture of gold") is applied to the Mother of God glorified in heaven. The theological language of Fol. 142r for Ode 9 captures the paradox of the impassible Word entering into passion (*стра́стемъ бы́въ стра́сть*, becoming the destruction of passions through His own Passion), reflecting St. John of Damascus's profound antithetical soteriology.

[^148]: Folios 142v–143r present the completion of Ode 9 across the three canons. In the first canon on Fol. 142v, the troparion cites Psalm 15:10 LXX / Acts 2:27 ("Neither wilt Thou leave My soul in Hades, neither wilt Thou suffer Thine Holy One to see corruption"). In the Cross-Resurrection canon, the Heirmos *Не рыда́й менѐ* (*Weep not for Me, O Mother*, celebrated on Holy Saturday) introduces the angel's proclamation to the myrrhbearers that Adam is raised and Hades despoiled. On Fol. 143r, Balaam's messianic oracle (Numbers 24:17: "There shall come a Star out of Jacob") is linked to Christ illuminating those held in spiritual gloom.

[^149]: Folios 143v–144r transition from the concluding troparia of Ode 9 of the Matins Canons to the Sunday Praises (*Хвали́тны*, Psalms 148–150). On Fol. 143v, the Virgin is hailed as a "divinely-woven loom" (*поста́въ бг҃отка́ненъ*, Greek ἱστός θεοΰφαντος) upon which the Word wove the tunic of His human flesh, deifying human nature. Fol. 144r presents the first four Resurrection Stichera followed by the Anatolian (*Восто́чны*) stichera, which evoke Psalm 18:6 LXX ("as a bridegroom coming out of his chamber") to celebrate Christ's triumphant exit from the sealed tomb.

[^150]: Folios 144v–145r contain the concluding Praises stichera, the rubric for the Evangelical Sticheron (*Стїхи́ра є҆ѵлїскаѧ*), and the opening of the Sunday Beatitudes (*Блаже́нны*) of Tone 6. In the upper margin of Fol. 145r, the folio numeral is typographically misprinted as *рм҃д* (144) instead of *рм҃є* (145), repeating the folio number of the previous recto. The polemical stichera on Fol. 144v challenge the Sanhedrin and soldiers over the empty tomb (Matthew 27:62–66, 28:11–15): "Either produce Him as dead, or worship Him as God" (*и҆лѝ ꙗ҆́кѡ ме́ртва предста́вите , и҆лѝ ꙗ҆́кѡ бг҃ꙋ поклони́тесѧ*).

[^151]: Folios 145v–146r complete the Sunday Beatitudes and open Sunday Evening Vespers (*Внеде́лю ве́черъ*) of Tone 6, which initiates the weekly liturgical commemorations (Sunday evening honoring the Bodiless Heavenly Hosts for Monday). On Fol. 146r, a notable 17th-century codicological feature appears: a printed slip has been pasted over the heading rubric to supply the missing model-melody indicator (*Подо́бенъ*), preserving `[...а́ггл]а́скїѧ пред̾идꙋ́тъ силы` (*The angelic hosts go before*). The second penitential sticheron incorporates the gospel parable of the barren fig tree (Luke 13:6–9, Matthew 21:19) as a warning against unfruitfulness and moral insensibility.

[^152]: Folios 146v–147r encompass the concluding Vespers stichera for the Bodiless Hosts and the opening of the Aposticha (*На стїхо́внѣ*). In the third angelic sticheron on Fol. 147r, the heavenly bodiless ranks are described as "formless and immaterial minds" (*безѡ҆́бразны , и҆ невеще́ственны ᲂу҆мы̀*, reflecting the Dionysian angelic hierarchy of Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite), created by the Father with the Word and the Spirit to hymn the thrice-radiant glory. The opening Aposticha sticheron draws upon the solemn dread of the Last Judgment and Christ's rejection of the unvigilant in Matthew 25:12 ("I know you not").

[^153]: Folios 147v–148r mark the conclusion of Tone 6 and the monumental opening of Tone 7 (Grave Tone / *Гласъ Седмый*). Fol. 148r initiates a new gathering (marked by signature `д҃ б`) with an ornate woodcut headpiece, a ligatured vyaz title (*ВЪ КНИГИ ВОСКРЕСНЫ*), and the opening Sunday Resurrection Stichera at "Lord, I have cried" (*Прїидѣ́те возра́дꙋемсѧ Го́сподеви*, based on Psalm 94:1 LXX). The hymnography celebrates the destruction of death's dominion and Christ's enlightenment of the human race in unison with the bodiless angelic powers.

[^154]: Folios 148v–149r contain the concluding Resurrection Stichera and the Anatolian (*Восточны*) stichera of Sunday Great Vespers in Tone 7 at "Lord, I have cried", culminating in the opening of the famous Dogmatic Theotokion (*Мати убо познася паче естества, Богородице*). The hymnography emphasizes the paradoxical twofold nature of Christ (first bodiless as the Word, then incarnate and put to death as man, rising in divine power), and David's prophecies in Zion (Psalms 49:2, 109:2 LXX).

[^155]: Folios 149v–150r conclude the Sunday Great Vespers of Tone 7 (with the completion of the Dogmatikon and the Aposticha stichera) and transition on Fol. 150r to the Sunday Midnight Office (*В неделю на полунощници*) with the opening of the Canon to the Holy and Life-Originating Trinity (*Канѡнъ святей живоначальней Троице*). Note the famous theological aphorism in the Dogmatikon: *идеже бо хощетъ Богъ, побеждается естества чинъ* ("for where God so wills, the order of nature is overcome"). The Midnight Trinity Canon is composed to the Heirmos *Маниемъ ти* (*By Thy beck*).

[^156]: Folios 150v–151r contain Odes 1 and 3 of the Sunday Midnight Office Trinity Canon in Tone 7. The hymnographer articulates high Trinitarian theology: the Father as the unoriginate Mind (*Оумъ безначалный*) begetting the co-eternal Word and causing the co-unoriginate Spirit to shine forth, and as the unoriginate root (*ѿ корене безначална*) from which the Word sprouts like a shoot (*стебль*). Christological typology is prominent in the Ode 1 Theotokion, where the Burning Bush (*купина*) seen by Moses prefigures the virginal conception without corruption.

[^157]: Folios 151v–152r span Odes 4 and 5 of the Sunday Midnight Office Trinity Canon, including the Sessional Hymn (*Сѣдаленъ: Помилуй, Троице святая*) after Ode 3. Note the antinomic Trinitarian phrasing on Fol. 152r: *нераздѣлно тя раздѣляемъ* ("indivisibly we distinguish Thee"), reflecting the classical Cappadocian synthesis of the undivided divine essence (*ousia*) existing in three distinct hypostases.

[^158]: Folios 152v–153r contain Odes 6 and 7 of the Sunday Midnight Office Trinity Canon, divided by the mid-canon Sessional Hymn (*Тажъ Сѣдална: Троице единосущная*). Fol. 152v displays notable technical theological vocabulary from St. John of Damascus's *De Fide Orthodoxa*: distinguishing the Persons solely by their hypostatic/existential properties (*различіе имуще точію, бытными коимиждо свойствы* — Greek ἰδιώμασι ὑποστατικοῖς), namely unbegottenness in the Father, generation in the Son, and procession in the Holy Spirit.

[^159]: Folios 153v–154r span Odes 8 and 9 of the Sunday Midnight Office Trinity Canon. In Ode 8, the refrains draw from the Song of the Three Holy Children (Daniel 3:57–88: *Вся дѣла Господня Господа пойте* / "Bless the Lord, all ye works of the Lord"). The hymnographer's personal petition on Fol. 154r asks the Lord to make the worshiper a "temple of Thy thrice-radiant ray" (*Храмъ мя твоея тріесіянныя сотвори луча*), safe from unseen spiritual demons and fleshly passions.

[^160]: Folios 154v–155r mark the completion of the Sunday Midnight Office Trinity Canon and the transition to Sunday Matins in Tone 7 at "God is the Lord" (*На Богъ Господь*). Fol. 155r features the seminal Resurrection Troparion of Tone 7 (*Разруши крестомъ своимъ смерть* / *Κατέλυσας τῷ σταυρῷ σου τὸν θάνατον*), accompanied by its Resurrection Theotokion celebrating Mary's perpetual virginity (*яже прежде рождества дѣва, и въ рождествѣ дѣва, и по рождествѣ паки дѣва*), followed by the first Kathisma Sessional Hymn (*Знаменану гробу* / *Τῆς ζωῆς ἐν тῷ τάφῳ*).

[^161]: Folios 155v–156r contain the second Kathisma Sessional Hymn, the Sunday Hypakoe (*Иже нашъ зракъ воспріемый* / *Ὁ τὴν ἡμετέραν μορφὴν ἀναλαβών*), and the opening Antiphons of the Tone 7 Sunday Songs of Ascents (*Степенна* / *Ἀναβαθμοί*). The text paraphrases Psalm 125:1–6 LXX ("When the Lord turned back the captivity of Zion... They that sow in tears shall reap in joy") and Psalm 126:1 LXX ("Except the Lord build the house, they labor in vain that build it"), reinterpreting the house allegorically as the "spiritual house" (*домъ душевный*).

[^162]: Folios 156v–157r conclude the Sunday Songs of Ascents (Antiphons 2 and 3), give the Sunday Matins Prokeimenon (*Воскресни, Господи Боже мой* / Psalm 9:33 LXX) and Gospel rubric, and open the Sunday Matins Canons of Tone 7. Antiphon 3 draws directly from Psalm 127:3 LXX ("Thy children like young olive plants round about thy table"). The opening Resurrection Canon is set to the Heirmos *Маніемъ ти на земны образъ* (*Νεύσει σου πρὸς γεώδη*), recounting the drying of the Red Sea (Exodus 14).

[^163]: Folios 157v–158r present the concluding troparia of Ode 1 for both the Cross-Resurrection and Theotokos Canons, followed by the opening of Ode 3 of the Sunday Matins Resurrection Canon in Tone 7. On Fol. 157v, the Cross-Resurrection troparion meditates on the "two life-bearing streams" (*Два источника живоносна*) flowing from Christ's pierced side (John 19:34: blood and water, typifying the Holy Mysteries of Baptism and the Eucharist). The Ode 3 Heirmos on Fol. 158r (*В начатцѣхъ небеса*) paraphrases Psalm 32:6 LXX: "By the word of the Lord the heavens were established, and all the host of them by the breath of His mouth."

[^164]: Folios 158v–159r conclude Ode 3 of the Sunday Matins Resurrection Canon in Tone 7 and feature Ode 3 of the Cross-Resurrection Canon (Heirmos: *Свышше силу* / *The power from on high*) and Theotokos Canon (Heirmos: *В начатцех небеса* / *In the beginning the heavens*). Notice the Christological imagery on Fol. 158v where Christ by His Cross quenches the flaming sword that guarded Eden (Genesis 3:24) and opens Paradise to the penitent thief (Luke 23:43). Fol. 159r meditates on the serpent's deceit in Eden, countered by the Son of God taking human flesh in the Virgin's womb to deify mankind through His tender mercy.

[^165]: Folios 159v–160r encompass Ode 4 of the Sunday Matins Canons in Tone 7. Heirmos 1 (*Очя недра не оставль* / *Not leaving the Father's bosom*) expresses the classical patristic paradox of the Incarnation: the divine Logos descends to earth without departing from the Father's bosom. The troparia depict the innocent Master submitting to scourging and standing before unjust human judges to free mankind from the condemnation of sin. The Cross-Resurrection Heirmos on Fol. 160r (*Смотряй* / *Foreseeing*) echoes the Prophet Habakkuk (Habakkuk 3:2 LXX), celebrating Christ's voluntary assumption of human form to slay the tyrant of deception.

[^166]: Folios 160v–161r present the conclusion of Ode 4 and the entirety of Ode 5 across the Resurrection and Cross-Resurrection Canons in Tone 7. Heirmos 5 (*Нощь несветла неверным* / *Night is unillumined to the faithless*) echoes Isaiah 26:9 LXX: "Out of the night my spirit awakes early unto Thee, O God, for Thy commandments are a light upon the earth." On Fol. 161r, the crucifixion narrative highlights cosmic convulsions at Christ's elevation upon Golgotha—the darkening of the luminaries, the earthquake, and the rending of the temple veil (Matthew 27:51), signifying the passing of the Old Covenant.

[^167]: Folios 161v–162r span Ode 5 of the Theotokos Canon and Ode 6 of the Resurrection Canon in Tone 7. Fol. 161v employs rich Marian typology: Jacob's ladder extending to heaven (Genesis 28:12) and the lily among thorns (Song of Songs 2:2). Ode 6 opens with the Jonah-typology Heirmos (*Плавающего во молве* / *Floating in the surge of worldly vanities* / Jonah 2:1–10), prefiguring the three-day burial and Resurrection. Fol. 162r portrays the souls of the righteous imprisoned in Hades awaiting Christ's descent, and the Apostles' astonishment transformed into proclamation when the Myrrh-bearing women announce the Resurrection.

[^168]: Folios 162v–163r conclude Ode 6 with the Cross-Resurrection and Theotokos troparia, and open Ode 7 of the Sunday Matins Resurrection Canon. Fol. 162v articulates Chalcedonian Christology: the uncircumscribed God the Word united Himself to humanity and became flesh from the Virgin. Ode 7 opens with the Heirmos of the Three Holy Youths in the Babylonian furnace (*Пещь отроцы* / *The furnace for the youths* / Daniel 3), singing praise to the God of our fathers. Fol. 163r notes how the Creator, Who brought all things out of non-existence by His divine counsel and effective power, shone forth from Mary's womb as the radiant dawn upon those sitting in the shadow of death.

[^169]: Folios 163v–164r contain the troparia of Ode 7 for both the Resurrection and Cross-Resurrection Canons, along with a solemn Trinitarian stanza (*Троична*). On Fol. 163v, the Pauline Adam-Christ typology (Romans 5:12–19; 1 Corinthians 15:21–22) is set forth: Adam suffered death through voluntary disobedience at the tree, but mankind is quickened through Christ's obedience on the Tree of the Cross. Fol. 164r celebrates the divine blood from Christ's pierced side cleansing the earth stained by the blood of pagan idolatry, followed by an explicit anti-Sabellian and anti-Arian Trinitarian confession (*Нераздельное естество* / *The undivided Nature*). The Theotokos troparion invokes Isaiah's vision of the live coal taken from the altar by the seraph (Isaiah 6:6–7) as a type of Christ borne in the Virgin's womb.

[^170]: Folios 164v–165r present the transition from Ode 7 to Ode 8 of the Sunday Matins Canons in Tone 7. Heirmos 8 (*Неопална огнем* / *Unconsumed by fire*) weaves together the Burning Bush of Mount Sinai (Exodus 3:2)—which revealed God to Moses of halting tongue—with the Three Holy Children preserved amidst the flames of Babylon (Daniel 3:57–88). On Fol. 165r, the hymnographer emphasizes that human flesh, which before the Passion was subject to corruption, was rendered incorruptible and made new through Christ's Passion and Resurrection, calling all created works of the Lord to bless and highly exalt Him unto all ages.

[^171]: Folios 165v–166r span the conclusion of Ode 8 and the opening of Ode 9 (the Megalynarion ode). Fol. 165v extols the pre-eternal Word born of the Virgin in the last times to save mankind. Ode 9 opens on Fol. 166r with the Heirmos *Нетления искушения* (*Giving flesh in exchange to the all-craftsman Word*), addressing the Virgin as the "vessel of the Incomprehensible, domain of the Uncontainable." A sharp anti-Monophysite and anti-Theopaschite polemic follows: let all alien-minded heretics be put to shame who ascribe suffering to the divine Nature; for the Lord of glory was crucified in the flesh, but remained impassible in His divine nature, being one Christ in two natures.

[^172]: Folios 166v–167r continue Ode 9 across the Resurrection, Cross-Resurrection, and Theotokos Canons, featuring a dogmatic Triadikon (*Троиченъ*). Fol. 166v warns against both Sabellian modalism ("we divide not the Trinity in essence, but confess It in Hypostases") and Arian subordinationism ("we sever not the Unity into distinct natures, but worship one Godhead"). It also confounds those who imagine a single composite nature in Christ (*единосложное естество*), demonstrating that He died as man, but as Creator of all rose from the dead. Fol. 167r recalls King David naming Mary the Ark of divine holiness (Psalm 131:8 LXX), carrying within her God Who sits in the Father's bosom.

[^173]: Folios 167v–168r conclude the Sunday Matins Canons and introduce the Sunday Praises / Lauds (*На хвалитех: Стихиры Воскресны Гласъ, з*). The initial stichera on Fol. 167v–168r celebrate the cosmic triumph of the Resurrection: "Christ is risen from the dead, having broken the bonds of death; proclaim good tidings of great joy, O earth; sing, O heavens, the glory of God!" Stichera 2 and 3 paraphrase Psalm 115:3 LXX ("What shall I render unto the Lord for all that He has rendered unto us?"), recounting how the Word became flesh and dwelt among us—the Benefactor to the ungrateful, the Deliverer to the captives, the Sun of Righteousness to those sitting in darkness.

[^174]: Folios 168v–169r conclude the Sunday Praises/Lauds in Tone 7 and open the Sunday Beatitudes (*Блаженны*). Fol. 168v articulates the Christological Stone typology, conflating Psalm 117:22 LXX ("The stone which the builders rejected, the same is become the head of the corner") and Isaiah 28:16 with the apocalyptic imagery of Daniel 2:34–45 (the stone cut out of the mountain without human hand prefiguring the virginal conception) and Daniel 7:13–14 (the Son of Man coming on the clouds of heaven unto the Ancient of Days). Fol. 169r introduces the Sunday Beatitudes in Tone 7, contrasting the fatal tree of knowledge in Eden with Christ as the Tree of Life whose fruit brings immortality, and invoking the penitent thief (Luke 23:42).

[^175]: Folios 169v–170r conclude the Sunday Beatitudes in Tone 7 and open Sunday Evening Vespers (for Monday). Fol. 169v features a dogmatic Triadikon emphasizing the identity of essence and distinction of hypostases ("Light is the Father, Light is the Son the Word, Light is the Holy Spirit; yet one Light is the Trinity, for God is one in three Persons"), followed by a Theotokion celebrating human deification through the Incarnation (*обожившеся тебе ради*). Fol. 170r begins Sunday Evening Vespers with compunctious Penitential Stichera at "Lord, I have cried" to the Podoben *Днесь бдитъ Іюда* (*Today Judas watches*), urging the soul to marvel at God's cosmic condescension in descending to earth to raise mankind from sinful misery.

[^176]: Folios 170v–171r encompass the Sunday Evening Vespers (Monday cycle) of Tone 7, featuring stichera to the Holy Archangels and Bodiless Hosts (as Monday is liturgically dedicated to the angelic orders). Following the Areopagitic hierarchy (*On the Celestial Hierarchy*), the Archangels are venerated as "secondary lights" (*свѣти вторіи*) shining by participation in the Primary Uncreated Light of the Trinity. At the Aposticha, the service transitions to the Lukan parables of repentance: the Prodigal Son (Luke 15:11–32) begging to be received as a hired servant, and the traveler beaten by thieves (Luke 10:30–37) seeking Christ the Physician of souls.

[^177]: Folios 171v–172r mark the transition between Tones 7 and 8. Fol. 171v concludes the weekly cycle of Tone 7 with a Marian Theotokion synthesizing major Old Testament types: the Bridal Chamber, the Closed Gate (Ezekiel 44:2), the Fiery Throne (Daniel 7:9), the Uncut Mountain (Daniel 2:34), and the Unburnt Bush (Exodus 3:2), sealed with an ornamental printer's palmette tailpiece. Fol. 172r opens Tone 8 with a primary Lavra woodcut headpiece across the page width, monumental Cyrillic ligatured vyaz lettering (*ВЪ КНИГИ ѠКТѠИХЪ ВСКРСНЪ / Гласъ О́смый*), and an ornate historiated initial 'В' introducing Saturday Great Vespers at "Lord, I have cried" (*Вечернюю пѣснь и словесную службу* / *An evening song and reasonable service* / Romans 12:1).

[^178]: Folios 172v–173r encompass the Anatolian (*Восточны*) Resurrection stichera at "Lord, I have cried" in Tone 8 and the monumental Saturday evening Dogmatikon (*Царь небесный* / *The King of heaven* / Baruch 3:38 LXX), traditionally attributed to St. John of Damascus. This Dogmatikon presents a definitive formula of Chalcedonian Christology, confessing Christ as "one Son, twofold in nature but not in hypostasis" (*единъ есть сынъ, сугубъ естествомъ а не составомъ*), truly perfect God and perfect man, born of the unwedded Virgin without human seed.

[^179]: Folios 173v–174r conclude Tone 8 Saturday Great Vespers with the Resurrection Aposticha and the Sunday Apolytikion/Troparion in Tone 8 (*С вышних сниде милосерде* / *Thou didst descend from the heights, O Compassionate One*). Fol. 173v articulates the patristic doctrine of Christ's triduum in death: Christ's divine hypostasis remained inseparably united to both His soul and His body upon the Cross, so that His rational human soul descended into Hades to despoil death while His flesh lay incorrupt in the tomb (cf. Psalm 15:10 LXX; Acts 2:27–31).

[^180]: Folios 174v–175r introduce the Sunday Midnight Office / Matins Canon to the Holy and Life-Originating Trinity in Tone 8 (Heirmos 1: *Вооруженна* / *Armed was Pharaoh* / Exodus 15:1–19). Fol. 175r incorporates the patristic interpretation of the Hospitality of Abraham at Mamre (Genesis 18:1–15), where the Patriarch receives the three mysterious visitors as an epiphany of the one Godhead in three divine Hypostases (*в трех поставех державу единственну разуме*). Ode 3 (Heirmos: *Утверждей небеса* / *Thou Who didst establish the heavens*) recalls Isaiah's vision of the Thrice-Holy Lord of Hosts upon His throne on high (Isaiah 6:1–3).

[^181]: Folios 175v–176r present the conclusion of Ode 3, the Sessional Hymn (*Седаленъ*) to the Holy Trinity (to the Podoben *Повеленное таинство* / *That which was commanded secretly*), and the opening of Ode 4 (Heirmos: *Ты ми Христе Господь* / *Thou art my Lord, O Christ* / Habakkuk 3:2 LXX). Fol. 175v articulates precise Nicene-Constantinopolitan Trinitarian dogma: the unbegotten Father (*нерожденный Отецъ*) eternally and incorruptibly begetting the Son as the radiance of His essence (*своего существа сіяніе*, Hebrews 1:3) and bringing forth the Holy Spirit as Light proceeding from Light (*Светъ от Света исходна*), consubstantial (*соестественъ*) and co-equal in honor.

[^182]: Folios 176v–177r encompass Ode 4 and Ode 5 of the Sunday Midnight Office / Matins Trinity Canon in Tone 8 (Heirmos 5: *Вскую мя отринул еси* / *Why hast Thou cast me off from Thy presence* / Psalm 87:14 LXX). Fol. 176v refers to the Virgin Mary as the "beauty of Jacob" (*Іаковлю доброту*, quoting Psalm 46:4 LXX) chosen by God from all ages. Fol. 177r expresses the classic Cappadocian dialectic of Trinitarian theology: distinguishing the Persons simply and uniting the Nature without confusion (*раздѣляюще просто, и совокупляюще*), followed by a heart-wrenching penitential stanza lamenting the soul's involuntary susceptibility to passion and spiritual alteration.

[^183]: Folios 177v–178r encompass Ode 6 (Heirmos: *Очисти мя Спасе* / *Cleanse me, O Savior* / Jonah 2:2–10) and the post-Ode 6 Sessional Hymns (*Седальны*) of the Sunday Midnight Office / Matins Trinity Canon in Tone 8. Fol. 178r presents a solemn dogmatic confession of the consubstantial Trinity, applying the negative adverbs of Chalcedon (*ἀσυγχύτως, ἀτρέπτως, ἀδιайρέτως* / *несліяннѣ, непревратнѣ, и нераздѣльнѣ*) to the divine Persons: confessing the unoriginate Father, the co-unoriginate Son, and the divine Spirit as one simple, holy, and co-equal Godhead worshipped alongside the heavenly hosts.

[^184]: In Fol. 179r (Page 181, right), the Lavra typesetter erroneously prints 'нединонача́ленъ, нединодержа́венъ' instead of 'є҆динонача́ленъ, є҆динодержа́венъ' (sole-ruling, sole-governing), mistakenly setting the initial Cyrillic letter 'н' in place of 'є҆'. The translation restores the canonical theological meaning.

[^185]: In Fol. 179v–180r (Pages 182–183), the Sunday Midnight Office Canon to the Holy Trinity reaches Ode 9, concluding with a petition for ecclesiastical unity and peace ('и҆ цр҃ква́мъ соединенїе'). The rubrics then transfer directly to Sunday Matins, referencing the Troparion from Saturday Great Vespers.

[^186]: In Fol. 180v–181r (Pages 183–184), the Sessional hymn Theotokion is the renowned megalynarion 'All creation rejoices in thee' ('Ѡ҆ тебѣ̀ ра́дꙋетсѧ...'), attributed to St. John of Damascus and sung in the Liturgy of St. Basil the Great. This is immediately followed by the Tone 8 Sunday Hypakoe ('Мѵроно́сица живода́вномꙋ...') and Antiphon 1 of the Anabathmoi (Ascents/Degrees).

[^187]: In Fol. 181v–182r (Pages 184–185), Antiphons 2, 3, and 4 of the Tone 8 Anabathmoi (Ascents) feature the distinctive Ruthenian Church Slavonic pneumatological strophes, confessing the Holy Spirit as co-enthroned ('сопресто́ленъ') and the peace-bestowing Lord consubstantial with the Father and the Son ('миропода́теленъ бг҃ъ бо, є҆́сть ѻ҆ц҃ꙋ̀ и҆ сн҃ꙋ є҆диносꙋ́щенъ гдⷭ҇ь'). The Prokeimenon follows Psalm 145:10 (LXX).

[^188]: In Fol. 182v–183r (Pages 185–186), the Matins canons for Tone 8 unfold in three distinct poetic streams: the Resurrection Canon ('Воѡрꙋже́нна фараѡ́на...'), the Cross-Resurrection Canon ('Во́дꙋ проше́...'), and the Canon to the Theotokos ('Пое́мъ гв҃и...'). At the bottom of Fol. 183r, the typographer marks a new gathering with signature 'Ѕ ѕ' (Cyrillic Dzelo = 6).

[^189]: In Fol. 183v–184r (Pages 186–187), the Sunday Canon Ode 3 draws on Matthew 12:29 and Luke 1:79 in depicting Christ binding the 'strong one' (Hades/Satan) and plundering his vessels ('свои́мъ бж҃твомъ крѣ́пкаго свѧза̀, и҆ совꙋ́ды є҆го̀ расхи́тилъ є҆́стъ'). The Cross-Resurrection ode then explores the Christological condescension of the Logos taking on Adam's form in obedience.

[^190]: In Fol. 184v–185r (Pages 187–188), Ode 4 of the Tone 8 Sunday Canon incorporates the Habakkuk canticle theme (Habakkuk 3:2 LXX) and the kenotic theology of Philippians 2:7 ('и҆стоща́нїемъ стра́ннымъ сни́де на зе́млю'). The rubric at the foot of Fol. 185r ('И҆́нъ І҆рмо́. То́.') indicates that the accompanying Theotokos canon shares the same heirmic melody.

[^191]: In Fol. 185v (Page 188, left), the text prints 'Бꙋ́рѧ тѧ̀ всечтⷭ҇аѧ... сл҃нце бо родила̀ є҆сѝ пра́вды' where 'Бꙋ́рѧ' represents an editorial or typesetting corruption for 'Зарѧ̀' (dawn/aurora), corresponding to the Greek 'Ὄρθрон σε πανύμνητε...' herald of the Sun of Righteousness (Malachi 4:2). Fol. 186r continues with the Isaiah canticle theme (Isaiah 26:9 LXX) in Ode 5.

[^192]: In Fol. 186v–187r (Pages 189–190), Ode 6 embodies the Jonah canticle archetype (Jonah 2:2–9 LXX). At the foot of Fol. 187r, the gathering transitions to signature 'З з' (Cyrillic Zemlya = 7). The Cross-Resurrection troparion contrasts Christ stretching His hands upon the Cross with Adam's outstretched hand reaching intemperately for the forbidden tree in Eden.

[^193]: In Fol. 187v–188r (Pages 190–191), Ode 7 of the Sunday Matins Canon opens with the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian Furnace (Daniel 3 LXX). The typographer sets 'Пѣ́нь Се́маѧ, І҆рмо́съ' on Fol. 187v, exhibiting haplography for 'Пѣ́снь Седма́ѧ'. The leaf closes mid-sentence on the Triadikon strophe on Fol. 188r with signature 'З з в҃' and catchword 'юще' (for 'вѣ́дающе').

[^194]: In Fol. 188v (Page 191, left), line 1 completes the Ode 7 Triadikon strophe begun on Fol. 188r catchword ('вѣ́да|юще'). Ode 7 continues with the Cross-Resurrection canon set to the heirmic melody 'И҆́же ѿ і҆ꙋде́а' ('He Who came from Judea', canticle of the Three Holy Children, Daniel 3 LXX), followed by the Theotokos canon. On Fol. 189r, Ode 8 opens with the Chaldean furnace canticle ('Се́дмь седми́цею пе́щь...'), marked by signature 'З з г'.

[^195]: In Fol. 189v (Page 192, left), line 7 exhibits haplography where the typesetter prints 'жи́знїю бо ме́ртвенное поже́рто бы́:' omitting the final cluster 'сть' from 'поже́рто бы́сть' ('that which was mortal might be swallowed up by life', 2 Corinthians 5:4). Fol. 190r continues Ode 8 of the Cross-Resurrection canon ('И҆́же на дре́вѣ рꙋ́цѣ мнѣ̀ просте́ршаго...'), depicting Christ calling naked humanity to warm itself in His noble nakedness upon the Cross.

[^196]: In Fol. 191r (Page 193, right), line 3 features the textual anomaly 'кра́комъ соше́дъ', an apparent typesetting error for 'зра́комъ соше́дъ' ('condescending in the form [of a servant]', Philippians 2:7, 'зра́къ раба̀ прїи́мъ'), highlighting the kenotic condescension of the divine Word before His glorious resurrection from the tomb.

[^197]: In Fol. 191v (Page 194, left), lines 7–11 preserve a distinctive historical-liturgical petition invoking the protection of the Cross and spear against the 'Ishmaelite people' (the Crimean Tatars and Ottoman forces who conducted recurrent raids across seventeenth-century Ukraine), pleading for the defense of the realm. Fol. 192r then begins the Sunday Praises (Lauds) Stichera of Tone 8.

[^198]: In Fol. 193r (Page 195, right), line 5 exhibits a clerical error by the Lavra compositor, setting 'ты́ же ꙗ҆́кѡ всесме́ртен бг҃ъ и҆ всеси́ленъ' ('Thou, as all-mortal God...') instead of 'безсме́ртенъ бг҃ъ' ('immortal God'). The translation emends this obvious erratum in accordance with standard Orthodox Christology.

[^199]: In Fol. 194r (Page 196, right), lines 6–7 contain a clear typesetting stutter and dittography: 'ра́дꙋйся ѡ҆ на́съ ст҃ая бц҃е: ра́дꙋйся ра́дои ся ра́до мі́рꙋ ро́ждшїя' for 'ра́дꙋйся ра́дость мі́рꙋ ро́ждшая'. Below this at line 10, an ornamental typographical frieze marks the formal transition from Sunday Liturgy Beatitudes to Sunday Evening Vespers (for Monday), commencing the penitential cycle.

[^200]: In Fol. 195r (Page 197, right), the Lavra typesetter erroneously set the running header 'ГЛАСЪ А.' at the top of the leaf, despite the fact that the text on the page belongs to Tone 8 Sunday evening ('гла́съ и҃'). The stichera are dedicated to the incorporeal heavenly hosts, whose commemoration occurs on Mondays throughout the Byzantine liturgical year.

[^201]: In Fol. 195v (Page 198, left), the rubric 'Въ пн҃елокъ на Оу҆́трени, по а҃ Каді́смѣ: Сѣ́: Гла́съ а҃' inaugurates the weekday cycle in Tone 1, confirming the codicological division of the 1629 Kyiv Octoechos wherein Sunday resurrectional services for all eight tones (fol. 1–194) are followed by the general weekday hymnography starting with Tone 1 Monday. The first Sessional draws upon Psalm 50:7 LXX ('Conceived in transgressions am I') and the parable of the eleventh-hour laborers (Matthew 20:1–16).

[^202]: In Fol. 196v–197r (Pages 199–200), the Sessional hymnody incorporates the Prodigal Son's repentance ('Make haste to open fatherly embraces unto me', Luke 15:11–32) and the Pauline meditation on the fearful judgment seat (Matthew 25:31–46). Fol. 197r line 12 introduces the Monday Canon of Repentance in Tone 1, set to the ancient Exodus heirmos 'Thy victorious right hand' (Exodus 15:6 LXX).

[^203]: In Fol. 197v–198r (Page 200), the Monday Matins office features the second canon to the Bodiless Powers, extolling the celestial orders as spotless mirrors receiving the illumination of the tri-personal Godhead and invoking the Logos as the 'Angel of Great Counsel' (Isaiah 9:6 LXX), before advancing into Ode 3.

[^204]: Codicologically and liturgically, weekday Matins in the Octoechos features two distinct canons chanted in parallel: the first canon is dedicated to the primary weekday theme (on Mondays, Repentance/Compunction), while the second canon is dedicated to the heavenly patrons of that day (on Mondays, the Bodiless Powers, i.e., the Angels). The heirmoi of the second canon are typically identical to or modeled upon those of the primary canon, indicated by the rubric *І҆рмо́съ то́йже* ("Same Heirmos").

[^205]: This Theotokion emphasizes the orthodox Christology formulated at the Ecumenical Councils of Nicaea (325) and Chalcedon (451): Christ possesses two nativities—an eternal, incorporeal generation from the Father before all ages (*и҆з̾ ѿц҃а нерожде́нна*), and a temporal, incarnate birth in the fullness of time from the Virgin Theotokos (*ржⷭ҇тво̀ и҆мѣ́я лѣ́тное и҆з̾ тебѐ дв҃о во пло́щеся*).

[^206]: The text reflects the Dionysian celestial hierarchy (*De Coelesti Hierarchia* by Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite), invoking the second triad of the angelic orders: Dominions (*гдⷭ҇ствїя* / *Kyriotētes*), Authorities (*вла́сти* / *Exousiai*), and Powers (*си́лы* / *Dynameis*), who contemplate and hymn the transcendent unity and tri-hypostatic essence of the Godhead.

[^207]: Codicological Note: In the 1629 Kyiv edition, the foliation on this right-hand page (Fol. 202r) is misprinted as *рч҃н* (198), repeating a pagination sequence from the preceding quire rather than advancing to *с҃в* (202). The liturgical and hymnographic text, however, is completely continuous and undamaged across the leaf boundary.

[^208]: Codicological Note: At the foot of this right-hand page (Fol. 203r), quire signature *Лл* (Quire 22) begins, indicating that Quire *Кк* (Quire 21) was configured as a four-leaf gathering (bifolium fold, leaves 199–202). Furthermore, the printed foliation on this leaf is misnumbered *рч҃д* (194) instead of *с҃г* (203), continuing the typesetter's foliation anomaly observed on Fol. 202r.

[^209]: The Heirmos of Ode 8 (*Во пе́щи* / "In the furnace") invokes the scriptural archetype of the Three Holy Youths in the Babylonian furnace (Daniel 3), a standard typological model of salvation through faith. The following troparion specifically addresses the personal guardian angel (*а҆́ггла твое́го смире́нїя*), assigned by divine providence to preserve the believer in Orthodox faith.

[^210]: The Heirmos of Ode 9 (*Ѻ҆́бразъ чи́стъ* / "Pure image") introduces the biblical canticle of the Theotokos (Magnificat, Luke 1:46–55). The first troparion employs the scriptural repentance model of the Canaanite woman (*ханане́а*, Matthew 15:22), pleading for deliverance from demonic torment and spiritual insensibility.

[^211]: The Exapostilarion (Photogogikon) to the Bodiless Powers is chanted to the Byzantine prosomoion melody *Жє́ны оу҆слы́шите* (*Gynaikes akoutisthēte* / "Give ear, ye women"). It invokes all nine orders of the celestial hierarchy: Angels, Archangels, Principalities, Dominions, Powers, Authorities, Thrones, Cherubim, and Seraphim.

[^212]: The Monday Matins Aposticha stichera draw upon classical evangelical parables of compunction, specifically contrasting the soul's spiritual inertia with the eleventh-hour laborer (*є҆динагона́десѧте ча́са мздꙋ̀*, Matthew 20:1–16) who receives divine mercy and reward despite late arrival in the vineyard.

[^213]: Liturgically, this rubric marks the transition at sunset from the liturgical day of Monday to Tuesday (dedicated to the Forerunner and Baptist John). While the overall Octoechos weekly cycle remains in Tone 1, the evening Idiomela (*стїхи̑ры самогла́сны*) at *Lord, I have cried* are set in Tone 2, reflecting the ancient practice of utilizing specific idiomelon melodies across the liturgical week.

[^214]: The Monday evening Vespers stichera (chanted at *Lord, I have cried*) initiate the liturgical celebration of Tuesday, weekly dedicated to the Holy Prophet, Forerunner, and Baptist John. Composed to the prosomoion melody *Е҆гда̀ ѿ дре́ва тя̀* (*Hote ek tou xylou se*), these hymns weave together the biblical titles of the Baptist: "seal of the prophets" (*пррⷱ҇кѡмъ печа́ть*), "voice of the Word" (*свѣ́тлыи сло́вꙋ гла́се* / Isaiah 40:3), and the Johannine proclamation of the Lamb of God taking away the sin of the world (*Сѐ а҆́гнецъ бж҃і́й* / John 1:29).

[^215]: Codicologically and liturgically, Tuesday Matins in the Octoechos contains Sessional Hymns (*Сѣда́льны*) centered upon compunction (*покаѧ́нны*) and the intercession of the Theotokos and the Baptist. Theotokion imagery on Fol. 210r draws directly upon the patristic doctrine of deification (*theosis*) formulated from 2 Peter 1:4: "Partakers of the divine nature have we become through thee" (*Бж҃тве́нномꙋ є҆стествꙋ̀ бы́хомъ прича́стници*), asserting that through the Incarnation of God from the Virgin, humanity is elevated into communion with the divine life.

[^216]: The Sessional Hymn following the third Kathisma versification contrasts the repentance of King David with the inertia of the soul. David's profound contrition over two specific transgressions—the adultery with Bathsheba and the engineered death of Uriah the Hittite (2 Samuel 11–12)—manifested in drenching his couch with tears (Psalm 6:6 LXX). The hymnographer laments that while David wept bitterly for two sins, the sinner remains dry-eyed and callous despite accumulating countless offenses.

[^217]: The first ode of the Canon to the Forerunner introduces the classical heirmos *Воглꙋбинѣ̀ потопѝ дре́вле* ("Of old He drowned in the deep"), referring to the destruction of Pharaoh's host in the Red Sea (Exodus 14). Troparia on Fol. 212r present the typological paradox of the baptism of Christ: the Forerunner, a created mortal, immersed into the waters of the Jordan Him Who is the unsearchable "Abyss" (*бе́зднꙋ*) of divine essence, thereby drowning the ancient flood of human transgression.

[^218]: The third ode of the Canon of Compunction incorporates the Pauline doctrine of the body as the temple of the Holy Spirit (1 Corinthians 6:19). The hymnographer prays that through the intercession of the Baptist—who resides in the heavenly sanctuary—believers may themselves be fashioned into living temples (*хра́мы и҆ на́съ сотворѝ дх҃ꙋ ст҃о́мꙋ*). The ode concludes with the classic Byzantine hymnographic antithesis: the Virgin mother suckles Him Who nourishes the entire cosmos (*дои́ши ма́теролѣ́пно, пита́теля всѣ́мъ*).

[^219]: Ode 4 of the Canon of Compunction employs the Habakkuk theophany heirmos (*И҆́же ѿ дв҃ы твоѐ ржⷭ҇тво*), based on Habakkuk 3:2–3 ("God shall come from Teman, and the Holy One from the thick and shady mountain of Paran"). In the Canon to the Forerunner on Fol. 213v, an explicit ktetorik petition is incorporated: the hymnographer petitions the Baptist on behalf of those who built and lovingly maintain his sacred temple (*и҆́же любо́вїю тѝ созда́ннаго бж҃тве́ннаго хра́ма*), beseeching that they be granted eternal life in the heavenly dwellings.

[^220]: Codicological Note: At the bottom margin of Fol. 215r (Page 217 Right), quire signature *Оо* (Quire 46) begins, demonstrating that the preceding quire *Нн* (Quire 45) was printed as a regular four-leaf gathering (bifolium fold, folios 211–214). The hymnography on this leaf emphasizes the Baptist's courageous defense of divine law before Herod Antipas, which led to his unlawful execution (*беззако́нно закала́ешися*, Matthew 14:1–12).

[^221]: Ode 5 of the Canon of Compunction incorporates the prophetic vision of Daniel 2:34–35, 45, in which a stone cut without hands from a great mountain smashes the pagan statue of composite metals—an established patristic type of Christ's virgin birth from the Theotokos shattering the idols of demonology. In the second canon, the Baptist is characterized as standing at the threshold of salvation history (*посредѣ̀ зако́на и҆ блгⷭ҇ти ста̀*), linking the Old Covenant Law with New Testament grace.

[^222]: In Ode 6, the Theotokion on Fol. 217r portrays the Virgin as the mystical Table bearing the heavenly Bread of Life (*Ꙗ҆́кѡ тра́пеза хлѣ́бъ вмѣсти́вши та́йный*), directly evoking Christ's discourse in John 6:32–51. The martyrikon troparia on Fol. 216v offer an epigraphic witness to the varied torments endured by early Christian martyrs, explicitly recording gouging of eyes (*ѻ҆́чїи и҆збода́еми*) and exposure to freezing winter frost (*померза́еми стꙋде́нїи лю́тѣ*), imagery characteristic of the Passion of the Forty Martyrs of Sebaste.

[^223]: The Heirmos of Ode 7 (*Херꙋві́момъ подо́бящеся дѣ́ти*) introduces the Canticle of the Three Holy Youths in the Babylonian furnace, drawing directly upon the Prayer of Azariah (Daniel 3:27–28 LXX: "For Thou art righteous in all that Thou hast done to us... for we have sinned"). The penitential troparion on Fol. 217v invokes David's classic confession from Psalm 50:5 LXX (*Во беззако́нїихъ гдⷭ҇и сѐ зача́тъ є҆́смь* / "Behold, in iniquities was I conceived"), anchoring the Tuesday morning compunction office in Davidic repentance.

[^224]: The Heirmos of Ode 8 (*В кꙋпи́нѣ мѡѷсе́и дв҃ческое чꙋ́до*) links the Old Testament theophany of the unburnt bush on Mount Sinai (Exodus 3:2) with the virginal conception and birth of Christ without corruption. In the preceding Theotokion (Fol. 219r), the hymnographer unites this typological Marian imagery with the Johannine witness: the infant John the Baptist, while still in the barren womb of Elizabeth, recognizes the incarnate Word in the Virgin's womb and leaps for joy (*бж҃тве́ннымъ взыгра́нїемъ* / Luke 1:41–44), thereby acting as the first prophet to confess Christ in the flesh.

[^225]: The Theotokion on Fol. 219v explicitly proclaims the patristic doctrine of deification (*theosis*), derived from 2 Peter 1:4 and St. Athanasius of Alexandria: "That God might deify us, having become incarnate of thy pure blood, and become earthly" (*Да̀ ѡ҆божи́тъ на́съ бг҃ъ, вопло́щся и҆́зъ чи́стыхъ кро́вій твои́хъ, и҆ бы́сть зе́менъ*). In the second canon to the Forerunner, the hymnographer petitions the Baptist—who physically laid his right hand upon the head of the Lord in the Jordan—to stretch forth that same right hand to lift up the sinner fallen in spiritual inertia.

[^226]: Ode 9 of Tuesday Matins combines two seminal biblical exemplars of repentance: the conversion of King Manasseh (*и҆́же манасі́ю ѡ҆браще́й согрѣши́вшаго*), whose apocryphal penitential prayer (2 Chronicles 33 / Odes 12 LXX) serves as a cornerstone of Byzantine compunction, and the nameless sinful woman / harlot (*спасы́й блꙋдни́цꙋ*) who washed Christ's feet with tears (Luke 7:36–50). The hymnographer models his prayer upon their contrition, begging for a continuous stream of tears to wash away his spiritual stains before the dread judgment-seat.

[^227]: In Ode 9 of the Canon to the Forerunner (Fol. 221v), the Baptist is acclaimed through a triad of botanical and aromatic biblical metaphors: the fragrant rose (*шипо́къ бл҃гово́ненъ*), the fragrant cypress (*кѵпари́съ* / Sirach 50:10), and the unfading lily (*неꙋвяда́емый крі́нъ* / Song of Songs 2:1–2), alongside honorable myrrh (*мѵ́ро чⷭ҇тно*), reflecting his ascetic purity in the Judean wilderness. In the subsequent troparion, an explicit ktetorik petition is offered for those who lovingly keep choir in the Baptist's sacred temple (*въ сщ҃тве́ннѣмъ хра́мѣ твое́мъ ликꙋ́ющимъ*), asking for divine deliverance and amendment of life.

[^228]: The weekday office on Tuesday concludes with the singing of the Beatitudes (*Блаже́нна*), a standard feature of the Studite and Ruthenian Octoechos tradition wherein the evangelical verses (Matthew 5:3–12) are interspersed with penitential, martyrial, and Johannine troparia. The concluding Triadikon (*Безнача́льный трⷪ҇ци тебѣ̀ покланя́емся*) provides an explicit dogmatic formula confessing the Holy Trinity as a simple, unoriginate, and tri-hypostatic essence (*є҆сттво́мъ тя̀ зна́я, є҆дини́цꙋ про́стꙋ и҆ трїѵпоста́внꙋ*), guarding Nicene-Constantinopolitan orthodoxy against Arian and Sabellian errors.

[^229]: On Tuesday evening, the liturgical cycle shifts from the commemoration of the Baptist to Wednesday, dedicated weekly throughout the Byzantine year to the Precious and Life-giving Cross and the betrayal and passion of Christ. The stichera at *Lord, I have cried* (Fol. 223v–224r) present the theological antithesis between Adam's condemnation at the tree of knowledge and his redemption through Christ's lifting up upon the Tree of the Cross under the legal curse (Deuteronomy 21:23; Galatians 3:13). The accompanying Staurotheotokion captures the maternal lament of the Virgin at Golgotha: "O my most beloved Child, whither has Thy light-bearing beauty set?"

[^230]: The Sessional Hymns of Wednesday Matins (Fol. 225r) incorporate the typological interpretation of Isaiah 60:13 ("The glory of Lebanon shall come unto thee, the fir tree, the pine tree, and the box together, to beautify the place of my sanctuary"), traditionally understood in Byzantine hymnography as the three woods of Christ's Cross: cypress, pine, and cedar (*На кипари́сѣ и҆ пе́ѵгѣ и҆ ке́дрѣ*). The preceding Aposticha sticheron (Fol. 224v) invokes Moses stretching forth his hands upon Mount Sinai/Rephidim in the cruciform sign to vanquish the host of Amalek (Exodus 17:8–16).

[^231]: Codicological & Rubrical Note: The rubric *По г҃ каѳі́ Сѣ̃ гл҃ по кра̃* (*Po 3 kathismi Sedalen glagolemy po krayegranesiyu*) indicates that following the third Kathisma versification, the sessional hymn is chanted in Tone 2 according to the poetic refrain or alphabetic/acrostic ordering. The accompanying Staurotheotokion on Fol. 226r portrays the Mother of God witnessing the voluntary humiliation and crucifixion of her Son, appealing to the boundless "bowels of mercies" (*за оу҆тро́бꙋ мл҃тей*) of the Redeemer Who takes away the sin of the world.

[^232]: The Matins Canon to the Precious Cross on Wednesday opens with the heirmos indication *И҆́же во́ды дре́вле, писа́нъ внѣ̀* ("He Who of old [gathered] the waters, written outside"), directing the chanter to find the full musical text in the external Heirmologion or the opening Sunday section. The hymnographer formulates a classic Pauline and patristic soteriological paradox: by accepting voluntary bonds in His incarnation and passion, Christ permanently bound the demonic adversary who had deceitfully ensnared humanity through the transgression of the commandment in Eden.

[^233]: In Ode 3 of the second Canon (to the Theotokos, Fol. 227v), the soul is likened to agricultural soil whose hardened furrows (*браздо́мъ дш҃а моеѧ̀*) must be softened and watered by tears of compunction in order to bring forth a hundredfold spiritual harvest, directly evoking the Parable of the Sower (Matthew 13:8, 23). The canon to the Cross on Fol. 228r formulates the foundational mystery of the communicatio idiomatum: Christ, Who in His divine nature is impassible (*є҆сттво́мъ бж҃твеннымъ безстра́стна*), voluntarily became passible in the flesh to bestow immortality upon mankind.

[^234]: The Heirmos of Ode 4 (*Положи́лъ ны* / *Polozhil ny*) opens the fourth ode of the Matins Canon to the Precious Cross. The subsequent troparia develop the Christological paradox of the Passion: Christ, Who in His divine majesty is above all celestial and earthly honor (*izhe chesti vsyakiya prevyshshi*), voluntarily endures blows upon His head with a reed (Matthew 27:30) and is crowned with thorns, thereby cutting down the prickly briars of ancestral sin and restoring human nature to its pristine dignity. Codicological note: quire signature `Сс г` appears at the bottom of Fol. 229r, which bears the early printed folio number `ск҃є` (225r), reflecting early misfoliation in the 1629 imprint.

[^235]: In Ode 5 (Fol. 230r), the hymnographer articulates the dogmatic definition of the Sixth Ecumenical Council (Constantinople III, 680–681) regarding the two natural operations or energies in the incarnate Word: Christ, though one from the Father (*ot Otsa Tya yedinago vedushchim*), bears two distinct operations (*deystvii dve nosyashcha*) corresponding to His divine and human natures. The two streams of blood and water flowing from His pierced side (John 19:34) serve as sacramental fountains of salvation. On Fol. 229v, the prophetic metaphor of the unrolled scroll (*Svitok*) derives from Isaiah 8:1 and Ezekiel 2:9–10, wherein Christ as the living Word is inscribed by the Father's finger.

[^236]: Ode 6 (Fol. 231r) presents a vivid eucharistic martyr-theology: the holy martyrs exposed to the flames are described as "baked like bread and offered upon the table unto God the King of all" (*yakozhe khleb ispetsayemi, i Bogu vsekh tsaryu na trapezu prinosimi*), directly reflecting the ancient patristic imagery of St. Ignatius of Antioch (*Epistle to the Romans*, IV). In the Canon to the Cross, Christ's voluntary piercing with the lance reverses the wound of the serpent in Eden, bestowing upon humanity "the ancient entrance" (*drevniy vkhod*) to Paradise.

[^237]: In Ode 7 (Fol. 232r), the hymnographer contrasts the cosmic convulsions attending Christ's crucifixion (the quaking of the earth, splitting of the rocks, and eclipse of the sun; Matthew 27:51) with the inner spiritual drama of repentance. The penitent petitions the Mother of God to dry up the "murky rivers of evil deeds" (*mutnyya reki lukavykh moikh deianiy*) through "streams of compunction" (*struyami umileniya*), that he may be directed toward the eschatological "waters of release" (*k vodam oslableniya*) at the dread judgment.

[^238]: Ode 8 (Fol. 233r) links the Piercing of the Side (John 19:34) with the revocation of the Edenic judgment: the turning flaming sword guarding the gates of Eden (Genesis 3:24) turns its back (*obrashchayushcheyesya nany oruzhiye, pleshchi dayet*), put to shame by the soldier's lance. The maternal lament of the Theotokos beneath the Cross (*nebes-chadnu yavi mya* — "make me not childless") underscores the reality of her maternal grief, addressing her Son Who preserved her virginal integrity even in childbirth.

[^239]: In Ode 9 (Fol. 234r), the hymnographer interprets the Parable of the Lost Coin (Luke 15:8–10) in an original Staurotheological synthesis: Christ is the seeker Who kindles the candle of His own sacred flesh upon the Tree of the Cross (*vzheg na kreste svoyu plot*) to find and recover the lost drachma of fallen humanity in the darkness of the world, thereby bringing joy to the celestial hosts. Quire signature `Тт д` marks Fol. 234r (printed `сл` / 230r).

[^240]: Ode 9 of the second canon (Fol. 235r) incorporates two distinct scriptural metaphors: the Pauline athletic exhortation to strengthen enfeebled hands and palsied knees for the spiritual race (Hebrews 12:12; Isaiah 35:3), and the moralized allegory of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10:30), wherein the sinner is ambushed by "spiritual thieves" (*razboyniki myslennyya*) who strip him of the garment of virtues. Quire `Уу` commences on Fol. 235r (printed `сла` / 231r).

[^241]: The Wednesday Matins Exapostilarion (*Oruzhiye nepobedimoye*, Fol. 235v) acclaims the Life-giving Cross with an elevated fourfold ecclesiological title: "the invincible weapon and confirmation of the faith, the great refuge and boast of Christians, the glory of the Orthodox, and the salvation of the faithful." The accompanying Aposticha stichera (Fol. 236r) pair the salvific Tree of Golgotha with the Edenic tree of disobedience, invoking Psalm 4:7 LXX (*Znamenasya na nas svet litsa Tvoyego*).

[^242]: The Beatitudes (*Blazhenna*) of Wednesday Matins (Fol. 236v–237r) alternate the evangelical verses of Matthew 5:3–12 with stauro-theological troparia. The hymnographer sets the Good Thief's confession (*razboynika zhe shchedre ispovedavsha Tya na kreste*) against Adam's transgression and expulsion from Eden, acclaiming Christ Who was numbered with the transgressors (Isaiah 53:12) and crowned with thorns in order to root out the thorns of the ancestral curse (Genesis 3:18).

[^243]: Codicological and modal rubric: On Wednesday evening (Fol. 237v), Vespers inaugurates the liturgical commemoration of Thursday (the Holy Apostles and St. Nicholas of Myra). The rubric explicitly directs the stichera to the Apostles at *Lord, I have cried* to be chanted in Tone 4 according to the prosomoion *Yako dobla* (*Os gennaion en martysin*, `гла д, Подо́бенъ, Ꙗ҆кодо́бла`), even though the Octoechos remains situated within the weekly Tone 3 section. The catalog of Apostles on Fol. 238r enumerates the Twelve, the Evangelists, and the broader synaxis of the Seventy.

[^244]: The Thursday Aposticha stichera in Tone 4 (Fol. 239r) celebrate the transformation of the apostles by the Holy Spirit at Pentecost: Christ revealed "unlettered disciples as teachers" (*neknizhnyya ucheniki kazatelya yavi*) who by their divinely inspired tongues overturned pagan delusion. The hymn draws upon the biblical witness of Acts 4:13 (*anthropoi agrammatoi eisin kai idiotai*), where the apostolic proclamation confounds worldly philosophy. Codicological note: quire signature `Ф҃ ф҃` marks the lower margin of Fol. 239r, which bears the printed folio number `сл҃є` (235r).

[^245]: On Fol. 239v, the concluding Martyrion of the Aposticha applies sacrificial and pastoral imagery to the martyrs: they are "living victims, rational whole-burnt offerings" (*zhertvy odushevlennyya, vsesozhzheniya slovesnyya*; cf. Romans 12:1) and sheep pastured "beside the water of rest" (*na vode pokoishchiney*; Psalm 22:2 LXX). Fol. 240r typifies Moses' passage through the Red Sea (Exodus 14) as a prophetical figure for the apostles cleaving the tumultuous waters of pagan polytheism and guiding the nations to the unoriginate Word. Quire signature `Ф҃ ф҃ в҃` appears at the foot of Fol. 240r (printed `сл҃ѕ` / 236r).

[^246]: The Matins Canon to the Holy Apostles in Tone 4 (Fol. 241r) opens with Ode 1 to the heirmos *Morya chermnaya* (*The Red Sea*). The hymnographer deploys the classic pneumatological metaphor of the apostles as "divine instruments of the Comforter" (*Bozhestvennyya Uteshitelya organy* / ὄργανα τοῦ Παρακλήτου), resonating with the breath of the Holy Spirit to chant a saving song. In the subsequent troparion, the hymnographer addresses the apostles as "eyewitnesses of Christ" (*Khristovy samovidtsy* / αὐτόпται τοῦ Χριστοῦ; Luke 1:2), begging their healing visitation for a soul paralyzed on the bed of sloth. Quire `Ф҃ ф҃ г҃` marks Fol. 241r (printed `сл҃з` / 237r).

[^247]: Fol. 241v introduces the second canon of Thursday Matins, dedicated to St. Nicholas of Myra. The abbreviated heirmos incipit *Трїсто.* designates *Трїста́ты крѣ́пкїѧ* (*Tristaty krepkiya* / Greek Τριστάτας κραταιούς; Exodus 15:4). Fol. 242r develops the ecclesiological typology of Psalm 45:5 LXX (*The streams of the river make glad the city of God*): the apostles are revealed as spiritual rivers whose teachings gladden the Church. Quire signature `Ф҃ ф҃ д҃` appears on Fol. 242r (printed `сл҃и` / 238r).

[^248]: In Ode 3 of the second canon (Fol. 242v), the hymnographer invokes the Lycian hagiographical tradition of St. Nicholas demolishing the pagan temple and statues of Artemis (*Artemide bezdushnyya kumiry*), transposing this historical action into an allegorical petition to destroy the soul's internal idols of passion. Ode 4 (Fol. 243r) interprets Habakkuk 3:8/15 (*Thou didst ride upon Thy horses...*) by acclaiming the apostles as Christ's "elect steeds" troubling the stormy waters of error, and as the sharpened arrows of the Mighty One (Psalm 44:6 LXX; 119:4 LXX). Quire `Х҃ х҃` commences on Fol. 243r (printed `сл҃ѳ` / 239r).

[^249]: In Ode 4 of the canon to St. Nicholas (Fol. 243v), the hymnographer recalls the saint's intervention in Myra on behalf of the three innocent men unjustly condemned to death by the governor Eustathius (*Nepravedno vedomyya na smert izbavil yesi*). In Ode 5 (Fol. 244r), the apostolic college is portrayed through the Johannine metaphor of the Vine (John 15:1–5): the apostles are branches blossoming with clusters (*lozy zhivotnyya, protsvetayushche grodiye*) that pour forth the wine of spiritual joy. Quire signature `Х҃ х҃ в҃` marks Fol. 244r (printed `см҃` / 240r).

[^250]: In Ode 5 of the canon to St. Nicholas (Fol. 244v), the hymnographer constructs an antithetical solar metaphor (*Zashel yesi yakozhe solntse...*): Nicholas sets like the sun in the West (in death / in his bodily rest), yet rises with radiant miraculous splendor throughout the Christian world. On Fol. 245r, Ode 6 depicts the apostles as the chosen sheep of the Good Shepherd who by faith transform the wolf-like savagery of the nations into sheep-like meekness, reversing predatory nature through the Gospel. Quire `Х҃ х҃ г҃` marks Fol. 245r (printed `см҃а` / 241r).

[^251]: In Ode 6 of the second canon (Fol. 245v), the hymnographer invokes the famous episode of the three imperial military commanders (Nepotian, Ursus, and Herpylion; *voyevody*) rescued from execution under Emperor Constantine through St. Nicholas's visionary appearance. In Ode 7 (Fol. 246r), the apostolic proclamation is described as a spiritual springtime that swiftly dispels "the winter of delusion" (*prelesti zimu razrushiste*), warming the faithful with the knowledge of God. Quire `Х҃ х҃ д҃` appears on Fol. 246r (printed `см҃в` / 242r).

[^252]: Ode 8 of the canon to the Apostles (Fol. 247r) utilizes the heirmos of Daniel in the lions' den (*Rutse rasproster Daniil*; Daniel 6:22) to depict the apostles as "fire-breathing mouths of Christ" (*usta ognedukhnovennaya Khristova*) and "divinely resounding trumpets" (*bogoglasnyya truby*) whose proclamation awakens souls from the grave of despair and delivers them from the jaws of the spiritual wolf. Quire `Ѡ҃ ѡ҃` commences on Fol. 247r (printed `см҃г` / 243r).

[^253]: In Ode 8 of the canon to St. Nicholas (Fol. 247v), the hymnographer commemorates the saint's championship of Orthodox doctrine at the First Ecumenical Council of Nicaea (325), where he silenced the blasphemies of the heretics and preserved the flock from "the ruinous pestilence of Arius" (*ot Ariyeva gubitelstva*). The hymnographer also incorporates an etymological play on the name Nicholas (from Greek *nikē* and *laos*, "victory of the people": *tezoimenit pobede*). Fol. 248r (Ode 9) appeals to the apostolic authority to bind and loose (Matthew 16:19; 18:18). Quire signature `Ѡ҃ ѡ҃ в҃` appears on Fol. 248r (printed `см҃д` / 244r).

[^254]: The completion of Ode 9 of the Thursday Matins canons (to the Holy Apostles and to St. Nicholas of Myra) on Fol. 248v–249r applies the architectural typology of Christ the cornerstone (1 Peter 2:6; Psalm 117:22 LXX: *Kamenie izbrannoye na polozhenago vo glavu ugla kamene*) to the apostolic foundation of the Church. Fol. 249r concludes with the opening of the Exapostilarion of the Apostles (*Petra i Pavla, Andreya, i Yakova, Vartholomeya...*), enumerating the apostolic college across the leaf break. Codicological note: Quire signature `Ѡ҃ ѡ҃ г҃` marks the lower margin of Fol. 249r, which bears the printed folio number `см҃є` (245r).

[^255]: On Fol. 249v–250r, the Thursday Aposticha concludes with the martyrion citing Psalm 115:6 LXX (*Precious in the sight of the Lord is the death of His saints* / *Chestna smert svyatykh Tvoyikh Gospodi*). Fol. 250r introduces the Thursday Beatitudes (*Blazhenna*) in Tone 4 with the classical patristic contrast between Adam's expulsion via the tree of transgression (Genesis 3) and the Penitent Thief's entrance into paradise via the tree of the Cross (Luke 23:42–43: *Dreva radi Adam raya byst izselen, dreva zhe radi krestnago razboynik v ray vselisya*). Quire signature `Ѡ҃ ѡ҃ д҃` appears at the foot of Fol. 250r (printed `см҃ѕ` / 246r), completing quire `Ѡ҃ ѡ҃`.

[^256]: Fol. 251r marks the beginning of quire `Ц҃ ц҃` (printed `см҃з` / 247r) and the liturgical transition from Thursday (Apostles and St. Nicholas, Tone 4) to Friday (Precious Cross, Tone 5). The concluding Beatitudes stichera include an explicit Trinitarian hymn addressed to the consubstantial and indivisible Trinity (*Troitse nerazdelnaya*), followed by a Marian theotokion honoring the Mother of God as the "unfading crown of the passion-bearers." The Friday Vespers stichera open under an ornamental typographical headpiece, invoking Psalm 95:12 LXX (*Then shall all the trees of the wood rejoice before the Lord* / *Vsya dreva dubravnaya da vozraduyutsya*).

[^257]: Fol. 251v continues the Friday Vespers hymnography of the Cross, venerating the instrument of redemption and citing Psalm 131:7 LXX (*Let us worship at the place where His feet stood* / *Mestu idezhe stoyaste noze Gospodni*). Fol. 252r introduces the Staurotheotokia, deploying the vivid eucharistic and botanical typology of the Virgin as the mystical Vine bearing Christ as the "unplanted Cluster" (*grozd nenasazhdenny*) wounded by the soldier's spear (John 19:34). Codicological note: Quire signature `Ц҃ ц҃ в҃` marks Fol. 252r (printed `см҃и` / 248r); the running header on this page mistakenly retains the previous section's `Гласъ, д҃` before the printer rectifies the headline to `Гласъ, е҃` on subsequent leaves.

[^258]: The compunctious Staurotheotokion on Fol. 252v–253r develops a profound interior dialogue of repentance: the soul's accumulation of sinful habits is likened to putrefying bodily wounds and sores (cf. Isaiah 1:6: *prilagayushchi bezumno svoim yazvam strupy mnozhayshaya*), urging immediate recourse to the Mother of God prior to the arrival of the righteous Judge. Fol. 253r concludes with the Stavrotheotokion to the model melody *Raduysya zhikonosny* (abbreviating *Raduysya, Zhivonosny Krestye* / Greek *Chaire zoēphore stauros*). Quire signature `Ц҃ ц҃ г҃` marks Fol. 253r (printed `см҃ѳ` / 249r).

[^259]: Fol. 253v features the Friday Aposticha sticheron in Tone 5 (*Gospodi, pri Moisei inogda prorotse*), paralleling the Old Testament type of Moses extending his hands to defeat Amalek (Exodus 17:11) with the Christian empire's victory under St. Constantine the Great through the sign of the Cross. On Fol. 254r, the Matins Sessional Hymn after the first Kathisma versification (*Mesto lobnoye ray byst*) transforms Golgotha into a new Eden where the planted Cross immediately produces the Life-giving Cluster. Quire signature `Ц҃ ц҃ д҃` appears at the foot of Fol. 254r (printed `сн҃` / 250r), marking the fourth signed recto of quire `Ц`.

[^260]: Fol. 254v contains the Matins Staurotheotokion evoking the cosmic paradox of the Crucifixion: the Virgin beholds stretched upon the Wood the very Lord Who "stretched out heaven like a curtain" (Psalm 103:2 LXX: *prostry bozhestvenne nebo yakozhe kozhu*). Fol. 255r commences quire `Ч҃ ч҃` (printed `сна` / 251r), advancing through the second Kathisma Sessional Hymns in Tone 5 which celebrate Christ's voluntary Passion as the total annulment of the ancestral culinary curse of Eden (*drevnyuyu klyatvu yazhe radi snedi*).

[^261]: Fol. 255v introduces the Friday Matins Canon to the Precious and Life-giving Cross in Tone 5 to the heirmos *Spasitelyu Bogu* (*Unto the Savior God Who guided the people through the sea with dry feet...*; Exodus 15:1). The preceding Sessional Hymn model melody *Sobe.* abbreviates *Ton synanarchon Logon* (*Sobeznachalnoye Slovo* / *The co-unoriginate Word*). The Theotokion bridging Fol. 256r and 256v sets the mystery of the Incarnation into high dogmatic relief, juxtaposing Christ as the "new Child" (*novo otrocha*) with His eternal identity as the Danielic "Ancient of Days" (*Vetkhi denmi*; Daniel 7:9). Fol. 256r is signed `Ч҃ ч҃ в҃` (printed `сн҃в` / 252r).

[^262]: Ode 3 of the Friday Canon of the Cross (Fol. 257r) is chanted to the heirmos *Siloyu Kresta Tvoyego* (*By the power of Thy Cross establish my heart*). The second troparion articulates the paschal typology of Exodus 12:7, 13, wherein Christ the slaughtered Lamb marks the spiritual doorposts (*pragi*) of the faithful's souls with His divine blood. The martyria depict the martyrs' sufferings as wine pressed from the fruit of the living Vine, gladdening the Church. Quire signature `Ч҃ ч҃ г҃` marks Fol. 257r (printed `сн҃г` / 253r).

[^263]: Ode 4 of the Friday Canon of the Cross (Fol. 257v–258r) is framed by the prophetic heirmos of Habakkuk 3:1–2 LXX (*Uslyshakh slukh tvoy i uboyakhsya* / *O Lord, I heard Thy report and was afraid*). The hymn contrasts the temporal setting of the "Sun of righteousness" (*solntse pravednoye*) upon Golgotha with the spiritual dawning of the "unwaning Light" (*svet nevecherniy*) upon creation. Fol. 258r completes the signed rectos of quire `Ч` with signature `Ч҃ ч҃ д҃` (printed `сн҃д` / 254r).

[^264]: Fol. 258v concludes Ode 4 of the Friday Theotokion canon and initiates Ode 5 of the Friday Canon of the Cross in Tone 5, chanted to the heirmos *Utrennyuyushche vopiyem Ti Gospodi* (*Rising early we cry aloud unto Thee, O Lord: Save us, for Thou art our God, beside Thee we know no other*; cf. Isaiah 26:9). Fol. 259r inaugurates quire `Ш҃ ш҃` (printed `с҃н҃є` / 255r), containing the martyria praising the indivisible fortitude of the martyrs whose bodies were severed by swords while their love for Christ remained uncut.

[^265]: Ode 6 of the Friday Canon of the Cross in Tone 5 is chanted to the Jonahic heirmos *Obyde mya bezdna* (*The abyss hath surrounded me, the whale became a grave unto me; but I cried unto Thee, the Lover of mankind...*; Jonah 2:3, 6 LXX). Fol. 259v troparion 2 weaves together the finding of the lost drachma (Luke 15:8–10) with the solar eclipse at Golgotha, describing the flesh of Christ ignited like a lamp upon the Wood to recover fallen humanity buried beneath passions. Fol. 260r is signed `Ш҃ ш҃ в҃` (printed `с҃н҃ѕ` / 256r), concluding Ode 6 and introducing Ode 7 with heirmos *V peshchi ognennoy* (*In the furnace of fire the holy Children sang...*).

[^266]: Fol. 260v–261r carries the conclusion of Ode 7 and the commencement of Ode 8 of the Friday Canon of the Cross in Tone 5 to the heirmos *Iz Otsa prezhde vek* (*Him Who was begotten of the Father before the ages, the Son and God...*; cf. Daniel 3:57). The second troparion of Ode 8 contrasts the proud demonic serpent cast down by the Tree with fallen humanity raised up through humble repentance. Signature `Ш҃ ш҃ г҃` on Fol. 261r (printed `с҃н҃з` / 257r) marks the third signed leaf of quire `Ш`.

[^267]: Ode 9 of the Friday Canon of the Cross in Tone 5 is introduced on Fol. 262r by the Marian heirmos *Tya pache uma* (*Thee Who beyond mind and word gavest birth in time to the timeless One...*). Troparion 2 directly cites Psalm 21:17–19 LXX (*Iskopasha rutse moi i noze moi, ischetosha vsya kosti moya*), linking the crucifixion between lawless men and the offering of gall and vinegar (Psalm 68:22 LXX) to the fulfillment of messianic prophecy. Quire signature `Ш҃ ш҃ д҃` on Fol. 262r (printed `с҃н҃и` / 258r) marks the fourth signed recto of quire `Ш`.

[^268]: Fol. 263r contains the Friday Matins Exapostilarion of the Cross (*Raduysya kreste presvyatyy, miru steno neoborimaya*), an expansive panegyric invocating the Holy Cross across ecclesial and civic estates (kings, warriors, apostles, faithful). The following Staurotheotokion on Fol. 263r articulates the maternal lament over the "primordial Light" (*svete nachalneyshiy*) setting upon Golgotha. The Aposticha Stichera of the Cross in Tone 5 then begin at the foot of Fol. 263r (printed `с҃н҃ѳ` / 259r).

[^269]: Fol. 263v–264r concludes Friday Matins with the Aposticha and Staurotheotokion portraying the Mother of God standing beside the Cross. The hymn explicitly cites the prophecy of Symeon from Luke 2:35 (*nynye bo ispolnisya glas izhe reche Symeon: serdtse tvoye neporochnaya oruzhiye proydet*), contrasting her painless virginal birthgiving with her agonizing compassion at the Crucifixion. Fol. 264r subsequently introduces the Friday Liturgy Beatitudes in Tone 5 (*Razboynik poznav na kreste* / Luke 23:42). Signature `Ш҃ ш҃ ѕ҃` on Fol. 264r (printed `с҃ѯ` / 260r) marks the sixth folio of quire `Ш`.

[^270]: Fol. 264v transitions from the Friday Beatitudes of Tone 5 to Friday Vespers (the commencement of Saturday of Tone 6), marked by the Aposticha/Lord I have cried rubric: *V pyatok vecher na Gospodi vozzvakh Stikhiry, svyatym Muchenikom, i svyatitelem, i Prepodobnym, Glas 6, podoben: Vse upovaniye* (*On Friday evening, at "Lord, I have cried," Stichera of the Holy Martyrs, Hierarchs, and Venerable Monks, Tone 6, model melody: Having placed all their hope*). The rubric opens with a decorative woodcut initial 'В'. Fol. 265r is signed `Ш҃ ш҃ з҃` (printed `с҃ѯа` / 261r).

[^271]: Fol. 265v includes cross-reference rubrics for Friday Vespers in Tone 6, citing the Dogmatic Theotokion *Kto tebe ne blazhit* (*Who shall not call Thee blessed, O all-holy Virgin?*) by directing the chanter to Tone 2 Sunday Great Vespers (*pn v v ko ve* = *poi v vtornik k vecheru* / seek in Tone 2 Vespers). Fol. 266r contains the Saturday Nekrosimon (*Nachatok mi i sostav*), an ontological hymn of Christian anthropology tracing human creation from earthly dust and divine inbreathing (Genesis 2:7), praying for the repose of the departed in the "tents of the righteous." Quire signature is omitted on Fol. 266r (printed `с҃ѯв` / 262r), representing the eighth leaf of quire `Ш`.

[^272]: Fol. 266v–267r contains Saturday Matins in Tone 6, opening with the first and second Kathisma Sessional Hymns. The Theotokion of the first Kathisma (*Izhe blagoslovennuyu narekiy matyer sebe*) links Christ's voluntary Crucifixion with the parable of the lost drachma (Luke 15:8–9), depicting Christ displaying the reclaimed soul of Adam to the rejoicing angelic hosts. Fol. 267r inaugurates quire `Ъ҃ ъ҃` (printed `с҃ѯг` / 263r), containing the Second Kathisma Sessional Hymns and the famous Nekrosimon *Voinstinnu suyeta vsyacheskaya* (*Truly all things are vanity, and life is but a shadow and a dream*; cf. Ecclesiastes 1:2, Psalm 38:7 LXX).

[^273]: Fol. 267v introduces Saturday Matins Canon of the All Saints (Martyrs, Hierarchs, Venerable Monks, Righteous) in Tone 6 to the heirmos *Chudotvornyy Farao potople* (*He Who marvelously drowned Pharaoh in the Red Sea...*; Exodus 15). The troparia celebrate the martyrs who put to shame the impious tormentors and arrived at the unwaning light of Christ.

[^274]: Fol. 268r continues Saturday Matins with troparia celebrating the holy women who overcame the demonic dragon by their manly ascetic struggles (*trudy muzheskimi*). It then introduces the second Canon for the Departed with the heirmos *Vodu proshed yakozhe sushu* (*Having traversed the water as dry land...*), whose rubrical tone designation in the Kyiv 1629 print gives `Гла́, и҆` (an evident misprint for Tone 6 / `Гла́, ѕ҃`, or referencing the standard Tone 8 heirmos model). Signature `Ъ҃ Ъ҃ в҃` on Fol. 268r (printed `с҃ѯд` / 264r) marks the second signed recto of quire `Ъ`.

[^275]: Fol. 268v–269r completes Ode 1 and contains Ode 3 of the Saturday Matins Canons in Tone 6 (Canon of All Saints to the heirmos *Na tverdem very tvoeya kameni* / *Upon the firm rock of Thy faith*, and Canon for the Departed to *Nebesnomu krugu* / *O Lord, Builder of the vault of heaven*). Fol. 269r is signed `Ъ҃ Ъ҃ г҃` (printed `с҃ѯє` / 265r), representing the third signed recto of quire `Ъ`. The troparia celebrate the martyrs who by their fortitude preserved the "divine sheep of Christ" and the holy women who joined the celestial choir before the Mother of God.

[^276]: Fol. 269v–270r contains Ode 4 of the Saturday Matins Canons in Tone 6, chanted to the heirmoi *Uslyshakh Gospodi slukh tvoy* (*I heard, O Lord, Thy report, and was afraid; I considered Thy works and was astonished*; Habakkuk 3:2 LXX) and *Tymi Khriste Gospod* (*Thou art my Lord, O Christ, Thou art my strength*). Fol. 270r (printed `с҃ѯѕ` / 266r) bears signature `Ъ҃ Ъ҃ д҃`, marking the fourth signed leaf of quire `Ъ`. The hymnography compares the holy ascetics to glowing coals (*ugliye*) consumed by love for God that burn away the material passions of this world.

[^277]: Fol. 270v–271r contains Ode 5 of the Saturday Matins Canons in Tone 6 to the heirmoi *Vozsiyavyy svet vsemu miru* (*Thou Who didst shine forth light upon the whole world*) and *Voskuyu mya otrinul yesi* (*Why hast Thou cast me off from Thy face, O Light unwaning?*; cf. Psalm 42:2 LXX). A noteworthy codicological development occurs on Fol. 271r (printed `с҃ѯз` / 267r), which introduces a fresh quire signature `Ы҃ Ы҃` directly following `Ъ҃ Ъ҃ д҃`, indicating that quire `Ъ` was set as an irregular 4-leaf gathering (binio) rather than the standard 8-leaf quaternion.

[^278]: Fol. 271v–272r spans the conclusion of Ode 5 and the commencement of Ode 6 of the Saturday Matins Canons in Tone 6, with Ode 6 chanted to the heirmos *Kitom pozhert* (*Swallowed by the whale of the abyss...*; Jonah 2:1–10). Fol. 271v contains an expansive eschatological supplication asking Christ to settle the faithful departed in the bosom of Abraham (Luke 16:22) where the voice of those who feast is sweet (Psalm 41:5 LXX). Fol. 272r (printed `с҃ѯи` / 268r) is signed `Ы҃ Ы҃ в҃`, the second signed recto of quire `Ы`.
